- mr president, evaluation in general and evaluation of education in particular is not a neutral process. it is always done to check the respondent's response to predefined objectives of the evaluator, who determines both the evaluation criteria and the consequences of whether or not the respondent meets them. the cooperation of the member states in the evaluation of education is becoming an important intervention mechanism in the education systems of the member states, with the aim of promoting an educational model far removed from universal, quantitatively and qualitatively extended general education. a model still far from the principle of ensuring equal opportunities for all. a model that costs little, but serves the need of the monopolies for cheap and above all obedient labour, perfectly adapted to their needs. this policy leads to a shrinking of general education in favour of training which produces disposable workers, even leading to the replacement of general education by an endless process of lifelong learning. it also leads to the removal of weak and usually poor pupils from systematic education to substandard training, as happened in greece, where pupils in the lyceum were reduced by 35% in a few years after the so-called educational reform. the evaluation is also intended to be used as a lever to push the school into a competitive mode where the best schools will gather the best pupils and teachers and the largest funding, operating on market criteria. this will lead to the degradation of the majority of schools, particularly in working class districts and the countryside. furthermore, through the process of self-evaluation, the responsibility that the state has for the quality of education is transferred to teachers, students and parents. if we really cared about the quality of education, we would focus our attention not on the rating mechanisms, but on improving the educational level of all people. the debate on how the evaluation will be carried out is useless and disorienting. what makes sense is to discuss the substance of the problem. what kind of man and what kind of society does school prepare for? if we judge the present school system by this criterion, we will surely conclude that it is far from meeting the real needs of workers and social progress.
- mr president, we must congratulate the committee on culture on the initiative it has taken in drawing up this report on how to protect cultural and natural heritage in the european union, and congratulate our rapporteur on the ideas and measures she proposes, with which i largely agree. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's financial instruments are used effectively and that the european union's financial instruments are used effectively. we are not going to replace national policy, obviously. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval. the reason is clear, mr president, because all the union's policies have a significant impact on the preservation and promotion of our cultural and natural heritage, usually in a rather negative way. the union often finances projects and interventions through the structural funds, for example, which cause damage or significant alteration to monuments, landscapes and other elements of this cultural and natural heritage. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. with great effort we have long since saved the ancient theatre of dodona from its proximity to the highway of egnatia, which would have prevented even the spectators from hearing the performances. the pressure exerted by many organisations has brought the case of the electricity pylons in the cyclades to the council of state, the highest administrative court in my country, which has, in a pioneering decision, once again recognised the value of the aesthetic landscape of these islands. the creation of the olympic rowing course on the marathon line, a historic site for the whole of europe, mr president, which is also a water habitat of particular value and which was originally included in the natura 2000 lists, is currently pending, madam commissioner. we'd like to get away with it, but i don't know how. the construction of the new acropolis museum is still pending, a short distance from the rock and the parthenon and on top of older archaeological monuments, threatening these monuments as well as the aesthetics of the entire space and the visual approach of the famous rock of the parthenon monument. these and many others have one thing in common: they seek or already have community funding. it is clear, therefore, why i strongly support the particular points in the resolution which call on the commission to consider before any project is financed the impact on the cultural and natural heritage, its historical weight and its aesthetic value, which must also be highlighted as criteria for the eligibility of projects.
- mr president, i should like to congratulate mrs de veyrac on having drawn up and highlighted the convention on the protection of the world cultural and natural heritage in the member states of the european union, and her report confirms the interest that europe must maintain in this important subject and, moreover, the initiatives it must take at world level. although nice did not take the crucial decision to free the cultural sector from unanimity so that we can take more decisive steps towards convergence, it is important that the report confirms that the european union is working to develop cultural diversity. it is also important in my view that the report shows europe's awareness that it is over-represented on the world heritage list and that this over-representation also applies to historic cities and religious buildings in relation to other categories of countries, to christianity in relation to other religions and denominations, to high architecture in relation to local architecture. this realization demonstrates that the concept of multiculturalism and respect for the foreigner is an ingrained idea and a central part of the thinking, thus enabling europe to play a substantial role in its relationship with the rest of the world in the era of globalisation. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment against the dangers of ionizing radiation is a very good one. i also believe - and i agree with mr papayannakis - that cultural impact studies should be a necessary condition for the approval of projects financed by community funds, so as not to cause disasters, which are often incalculable. it is a good thing that the culture 2000 programme has devoted one third of its total budget to cultural heritage, archaeology, underwater, architecture, museums, libraries, archives, thus stimulating national actions. however, as the report points out, emphasis should also be placed on the rural heritage, the ancient or paleolithic forests, the habitats and wetlands, i.e. those considered to be more or less marginal. education and training in all professions related to the protection, conservation and exploitation of the cultural and natural heritage is also of great importance, together with the awareness-raising of students and young people and the development of a volunteering movement that can make a decisive contribution.

in writing. - (el) mrs de veyrac's report is a good report and contains some constructive proposals. that is why we, as members of the european parliament from the communist party of greece, voted in favour of this report. however, we are very concerned that the proposals will not be addressed to ears that do not want to hear. everyone knows that cultural and natural heritage is threatened with complete disappearance by activities that degrade or destroy the urban and topographical environment that houses invaluable elements of such heritage. excessive and unregulated land use, the development of buildings in the historic centres of towns and cities, in green spaces and open areas, the lack of effective measures to protect forests and the natural environment are the consequences of an increasingly violent attack on the natural and cultural landscape. all this, of course, is not the result of a few malicious people, but the direct result of a policy which commercialises everything with the sole criterion of ensuring an ever greater profit. a typical example is the tvx gold plant in olympiada, chalcidice, near stagira, aristotle's hometown, which has destroyed the forest and endangered the monuments; and as if that wasn't enough, the people of the region were condemned for daring to defend their historic and natural environment. many examples could be drawn from the devastation perpetrated or planned under the pretext of hosting the 2004 olympic games in athens. the european union not only does nothing to prevent and combat such phenomena, but on the contrary is the main supporter of this policy. it finances works that are built on archaeological sites by burying and razing ancient monuments, as in the case of the acropolis museum. at the moment, the greek government is abrogating article 24 of the constitution and at the same time, it is abolishing the legislative protection, however weak and rudimentary, that applied to forests, without any reaction from the eu. the eu is full of fancy words about culture and, while proclaiming that cultural heritage is a fundamental element of the specific identity and historical evolution of a society, it has done nothing to return the parthenon marbles to the natural setting of which they are an inseparable part - the minimal concrete proof of respect for cultural and historical heritage and protection of historical memory. it also remains indifferent to the looting of monuments in northern cyprus and their barbaric destruction by the turkish occupation troops. finally, it bears a huge responsibility for the destruction of monuments caused by the bombing raids on yugoslavia. this situation cannot be dealt with by half measures, wishful thinking or, indeed, by the logic of the market. we must mobilize all the popular forces, all the cultural bodies, all the workers, we must impose a different policy which respects human labour and the results of its creation.

in writing. - (el) as members of the communist party of greece, we will vote against the report by mr bushill-matthews because the concrete measures proposed therein - financial assistance for innovative and job-creating small and medium-sized enterprises (smes) - are part of the general policy of monopolies and the eu towards smes; the policy is based on three main lines: selective aid granted to a small number of small and medium-sized enterprises (in the light of the greek economy) with a view to consolidating their role as satellites of the monopolies and strengthening the processes of concentration of capital and production. · to increase the conditions of competition for the overwhelming majority of small but also for the remaining mediumsized enterprises, with a view to strengthening the same processes by other means. · furthermore, in all eu programmes for smes, it is clear that monopoly capital is seeking to "unload" on smes the jobs it is obliged to eliminate, with the increased exploitation of the working class, aided in this by technological developments. this is an attempt which has little chance of success because it stumbles upon the contradictions of the capitalist system. it is also worth noting that the beneficiaries of european programmes are individual enterprises and not all smes. it goes without saying that big business knows that a policy favouring all smes would create a competitor with whom it would have to reckon. thus, it is not possible to take advantage of the aid granted under the european programmes, even if it is granted to small enterprises, since the position of each of these, taken separately on the capitalist market, is extremely precarious. a number of observations on the content of the programmes in question reinforce the general observations above: 1. the the ceilings for the enterprises targeted by these measures (250 employees and 14 billion drachma in annual turnover) allow, by deduction, large enterprises to benefit from them, in the light of greek data. in greece, 91% of enterprises employ up to 4 employees and have a turnover of up to drachma 60 million. in this respect, the finding in the european parliament's report that there is no concern for small businesses is well founded. 2. the the total number of smes targeted by these measures is negligible compared to the total number of smes in the eu. in concrete terms, this means 7 387 smes out of a total of around 15 million for the eu, or 0.05%, within two years of their implementation. 3. the the overall amounts allocated by the european funds are desperately low and do not give substance to the notion of a genuine policy in favour of smes. in total, 273 million euros (93.6 billion drachmas) have been allocated, or 18 euros (6.170 drachmas) per company! 4. the the situation is even worse in terms of the results of this policy: in the end, 2 238 jobs were created for the tens of thousands of unemployed in the eu, at a cost of 122 000 euros (42 million drachmas) per job. the fifth. for 97% of smes that benefited from these measures, it is the third part of the programmes concerning the guarantee of financing that the enterprises themselves have assumed in full. it is therefore not surprising that such a complex, costly and poorly-resulting procedure should be presented as a success of eu policy. greece was not included in any of these programmes and therefore did not receive any funding. for all these reasons, we shall vote against the report in question and against the commission's proposal.

the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. the commission is well aware of and deeply regrets the fact that people have died and been injured as a result of fires and expresses its sympathy to the families of the victims. the commission also regrets the environmental and economic damage caused by fires. this is particularly important at the present time, in view of the christmas fires in the netherlands and china, which killed many young people. the commission considers that coordination of fire safety issues is of great importance. improving fire safety in the community, and in particular fire prevention, is a matter of high priority in the programme of community action in favour of the rescue services or civil protection. i can also inform you that the construction industry unit of the directorate-general for enterprise has recently launched a study on fire safety in the european union in relation to construction. this study aims to improve the harmonisation of building standards so that buildings across the union meet the same safety requirements. the construction products directive lays down essential requirements for all types of buildings, including tunnels. the commission is also assessing, in close cooperation with the member states, the impact of council recommendation 86/666 on hotel fire safety. in this area, the commission plans to present a report to the european parliament and the council in the first half of this year. it must also be borne in mind that member states can set their own safety requirements and that fire prevention measures are primarily the responsibility of the member states. however, this is a global problem which is taken into account in community policies in various fields to complement the measures taken in the member states. there may still be some weaknesses in the coordination of fire safety issues. to remedy this and to improve coordination, the emergency services unit of my own directorate-general and the relevant departments in the industrial, consumer and health sectors set up a working group in october last year, including representatives of the various services.
it is easy for me to agree with mr färm on these points, of course. but it is not so easy because we are also dealing with a reality which is very different from country to country and where there is often a system which is highly decentralised down to the municipal level. it is therefore obvious that it can be difficult to issue recommendations, and even more difficult with a directive that would apply virtually everywhere and clarify all these aspects. we are trying, as i said in my first speech, to coordinate as much as possible. we are also trying to see if we can get standards that are similar across the borders of the member states and make sure that we coordinate our own efforts. much can be improved by the fact that we now have a civil protection unit which will be the one with such overall responsibility. we have to continue the somewhat laborious process of reviewing special regulations on buildings, discos, boats or whatever it may be to make sure that we are getting better and more efficient in our coordination of fire operations.
- commissioner, i would like to point out to you - and i have pointed this out to the house before - that in the event of natural disasters such as fires, floods and earthquakes, it is important for the state to intervene quickly to deal with the problem and, above all, to save lives. local forces in each particular country are never enough. it would be significant if there was a plan of cooperation between all the local emergency services, so that in the event of an earthquake, a flood, a fire in one country, all the forces from all the countries that have no problem would immediately rush to save at least the people. have you considered this as an idea that could be promoted and have some effectiveness?

in writing. - (el) the report by the committee on agriculture and rural development is moving in the right direction. financial support is needed for the establishment of young farmers to help stop the exodus to the cities. young farmers can be the pivot of the renovation of deserted regions, small islands, traditional communities that are dying. it is important, with the use of new technologies such as the internet, to create a new, technically advanced, human-sized cultural environment for their work. now, one aspect which is not included in the report of the committee on agriculture and which is important, given the impasse caused by over-intensive farming which led to mad cow disease, is to create for young farmers conditions favourable to the development of alternative and organic family farming.
in writing. - (el) my group voted in favour of the resolution. i must, however, emphasize one particular point. under current community legislation, the exclusive right to fish for greek fishermen has been set at 6 nautical miles instead of the 12 miles applicable to other eu countries, in particular mediterranean countries. under these conditions, greek fishermen are not treated equally and are at a disadvantage compared to other countries under the common fisheries policy. since, under the declaration referred to in article 5 (1) of annex ix to the united nations convention on the law of the sea, the community has exclusive competence with regard to the conservation and management of marine fisheries resources and is therefore empowered to adopt the relevant rules and provisions, the commission should, by using the abovementioned competence, also define for greek fishermen the exclusive right to fish within the limit of 12 nautical miles. similarly, in its resolution a4-0018/99 (c-153/1-6-99), the european parliament calls on the commission to propose the extension of the exclusive economic zone for fisheries to 12 nautical miles in order to ensure an appropriate policy of decentralisation and protection of the interests of coastal areas. the commission should adopt the above decision of the european parliament.
in writing. - (el) the joint resolution on the 'balkan syndrome' serves only one purpose: to cover up the political crime of nato and the european union, which, in the name of a so-called defence of uranium, although they knew very well, from the war against iraq, its long-term deadly effects. the joint resolution, while refusing to accept a causal link between depleted uranium and the cases of cancer and leukaemia among military personnel who were part of the occupation forces in bosnia and kosovo, as well as those who took part in the gulf war, merely expresses the wish for "transparent discussions" and "the establishment of working groups", and calls on "nato member states to propose a moratorium on the use of depleted uranium weapons", while "inviting nato to consider the use of other types of munitions", i.e. to continue to kill, but with "own weapons". it is symptomatic that the amendment proposal of the united left group calling for a ban on the production, testing, use and sale of depleted uranium weapons was triumphantly rejected by an overwhelming majority of members of the european parliament, receiving only 23% of the votes. the european parliament has certainly been consistent with itself, since, as the war criminal, former nato secretary-general and now high representative of the eu, javier solana, cynically pointed out in his speech, it was the european parliament itself which insistently called for military intervention in the balkans, both in bosnia and in kosovo. it would, of course, be an illusion to expect war criminals to condemn themselves. balkans and for the cost of care and compensation for the victims and the clean-up of the environment to be charged to nato and the eu. as meps of the kke (communist party of greece), we voted against the joint resolution and call on the peoples to organise and develop their own resistance and response to the barbarism of the new world order.

i have to say that i am not in favour of this. - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is a fact that the recognition of women's human rights and fundamental freedoms is a very recent development. it was only in the 20th century that women won the right to access education, the sciences, the arts, paid employment and were finally recognized as entities with political rights. the 20th century began with the struggle of women to win the right to vote. at the beginning of the 21st century, the demands of half the world's population are focused on achieving a fair distribution between the sexes of all responsibilities and obligations in all areas of economic, social, family and political life. the need to promote women in decision-making processes and to ensure a balanced participation of women and men in democratic institutions, reflecting the gender balance that nature has provided, is a prerequisite for democracy and peace, for the cohesion and competitiveness of the european union. in response to this need and on the basis of the evidence-based proposals of the european network of experts set up by the commission in 1992, the council adopted in december 1996 an important recommendation to the member states to promote the balanced participation of women and men in decision-making processes. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the best performing countries are those with a long tradition of promoting gender equality, such as sweden and finland, with women in parliament and government accounting for over 40%. sweden, as you know, has a government with 10 women and 10 men ministers. of course, on the other side of the river are countries like greece and italy with female participation rates that are below or around 10%. the problem, of course, is not so much the low levels of administration as the high places where the real responsibilities of decision-making, of exercising power, of planning and organizing the future of the european union lie. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the social partners are able to take part in the negotiations, and i am sure that the commission will be able to do so. we need a new institutional and social framework which fully reflects the changes that have taken place to date. ensuring equal opportunities and sharing responsibilities fairly and equitably between the sexes so that decisions reflect the desires, values, priorities, interests and abilities of both sexes of humankind. today, with women fully involved in the production process and their participation in european universities higher than that of men, it is not justified to exclude them from the centres of policy and decision-making, nor is it fair for women to bear the full burden of family obligations and housework alone. the cooperation and co-decision of the two sexes, the balance of the female and the male, according to studies and research, can result in a more balanced and fair world. the balance, of course, cannot be lower than 40% for either sex, a figure which has been accepted, particularly by the nordic countries, and has been in place for years. it is certain that a critical mass of women in decision-making centres would put on the political agenda, as priority issues, issues related to quality of life, such as environmental protection, social policy and welfare, health care, education, the fight against drug abuse and trafficking, the resolution of disputes by peaceful means. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done. the legislation on public funding of parties should provide additional funds for parties that ensure gender balance in party combinations and provide for sanctions for those parties that have a low number of women, as france recently decided. governments, particularly those of countries where women's participation is less than 30%, should consider adapting or reforming their electoral systems. the introduction of quotas should also be accompanied by other measures concerning parties and the adjustment of the electoral system if they are to be effective. it is also necessary to address stereotypes through education and gender roles from an early age and to develop women's capacity to participate in leadership positions. the social infrastructure for care and support for the elderly needs to be strengthened and the member states need to include amendments to the treaty at the next intergovernmental conference in order to have a strong legal basis to ensure balanced participation of the two sexes in the institutions of the european union, so that the obvious is finally achieved, namely that the natural balance of the sexes is also expressed in the centres of power, so that decisions on the future of europe are not taken in the absence of women.

commission. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, before i turn to the main point which i have put to you this morning on behalf of the commission, i should like to say a few words, if i may. madam president, i echo the concerns expressed earlier by mrs figueiredo, secretary of state torres marques and minister cunha following the very serious natural disasters which have struck the norte region of portugal. since i am in charge of these matters, i want to tell them that we will naturally use all the possibilities of redeploying appropriations as we have done in other circumstances and in other natural disasters in europe to deal with the exceptional reconstruction, repair or prevention expenditure caused by these disasters alongside the portuguese authorities. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the european commission devoted most of its debate this morning to approving the report, the second cohesion report on economic and social cohesion in the union, which i had the honour of presenting to the commission in agreement with my colleagues mrs diamantopoulou and mr fischler. i presented this report under article 159 of the treaty, which you are well aware of, as monica wulf-mathies did in 1996, and it was as a result of this 1996 report that the first elements were drawn up to prepare agenda 2000. the report i am presenting to you, a full summary of which is available in three languages at the entrance to your hall, for the first time in its first part, gives a snapshot of the situation of the union from the point of view of cohesion for the 27 countries of the enlarged union. in a second part, it examines the impact of other community policies on the union's objective. it takes stock of the results of the structural funds and the cohesion fund. finally, he outlines a number of ways forward or recommendations for the future in a synthesis document which, i repeat, is being circulated to each of you. the whole report, ladies and gentlemen, will be available on the internet this evening, together with all the maps and figures attached to it. i would like to mention two points in this keynote speech before i listen to you: what are the challenges to cohesion in this enlarged union and how can we meet these challenges? the first challenge i will mention is that of a global context which is changing rapidly and profoundly for each of our countries and therefore for the union: the challenge of globalisation, the challenge of new activities based on intelligence and knowledge, the new economy, the information society, the challenge of demographic structure. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the second issue is enlargement. the union of 27 and perhaps 28 or more countries is becoming our new horizon and what is clear, when you read this report in particular, is that there will be more disparities, more inequalities in this enlarged union and in a dimension that has never been seen in any of the previous enlargements. the european union's population will increase by 30%, its area will increase by 30%, and yet the union's gross domestic product will increase by only 5%. that is why we have identified in this report a third group of countries which will include eight of the candidate countries, eight, which will represent the poorest 16% of the population of the enlarged union, and whose per capita income will be only 40% of the community average. this is the real challenge and the real change. and so, with enlargement, the centre of gravity of cohesion policy will shift to the east. of the 105 million inhabitants of the candidate countries, 98 million will live in those regions whose level of gross domestic product per capita is below 75% of the average for the enlarged union. however, ladies and gentlemen, just because we are going to bring the very poor regions of the east into the union, does not mean that the present poor regions of the south, the north, the centre, and i am also thinking of the peripheral or ultra-peripheral regions, will become, as if by magic, as if by a stroke of a magic wand, prosperous and rich all at once. these regions which are currently in objective 1, although we have seen progress, still have problems. there's been some progress. the per capita income of these regions has approached the community average. it rose from 63% in 1988 to 69% in 1997. partly thanks to the structural funds and the cohesion fund, the cohesion countries have experienced economic growth above the community average. however, within a growing number of member states, the trend is not towards a reduction in disparities. inequality persists and even worsens. unemployment in some regions is above 20%, some regions have long been lagging behind because of their peripheral location, desertification, and women's employment is 19 points below that of men. this is the observation you will read in this report, ladies and gentlemen, at the same time as we describe territorial inequalities, pockets of poverty, and sometimes extreme poverty, in cities or in certain rural areas. how do we respond to these challenges? ladies and gentlemen, cohesion policy must remain, in our view, the central element of the european union's model of society. we are in a european union which wants to organise its solidarities. this means asking three questions about cohesion policy: why, for whom, how? i hear people say a few times: 'we cannot do everything from brussels, we need more subsidiarity. i am in favour of more subsidiarity. but i do not agree and i will not agree that cohesion policy should become a mere financial transfer policy. we cannot simply send cheques, we cannot accept any renationalisation of cohesion policy. we have common priorities, real community priorities, and it is in our common interest to develop a framework for cohesion policy in a sustainable and balanced way. at this point the debate is just beginning. i have no decision to hand you over. maybe it was just a thought. one of these reflections is based on the experience gained over more than ten years in the context of cohesion policy, and it leads me to believe that there are more crucial, more serious issues than others for the development of our territories. i have mentioned, for example, the urban question, networking, or the specific questions affecting areas with geographical handicaps, remoteness, altitude and natural handicaps. we must also think about human resources, because this is employment-related. our report includes guidelines for more and better jobs, for better social inclusion and for equal opportunities. finally, in the light of this debate, ladies and gentlemen, the commission may be prepared, in due course, to propose to the other community institutions a more strategic and coherent approach to the development of the union's territories. the objective of economic and social cohesion, which remains our objective, would thus have a more territorial dimension and basis. cohesion policy for whom? i have two convictions on this subject, which have been shared by the whole commission. the first is that we must maintain priority for the regions whose development is lagging behind, in the future member states as well as in the present countries of the union. the problem of priority support for the most backward regions naturally raises the question of the level at which we will have to set the eligibility threshold for these regions whose development is lagging behind. my second conviction is that the regions whose development is lagging behind are not the only ones facing difficulties to which we will have to find a solution for the other regions in the enlarged union. because we have community priorities, we must think of action throughout the union, because often within the same state there are regions richer than others and sometimes even within the same region there are exclusion zones next to areas of dynamism - that is to say, as the urban audit i published a few months ago has shown. as regards implementation, ladies and gentlemen, i think it is more possible to delegate to the member states by applying the principle of subsidiarity better than in the past, provided that we are sure that the community's priorities are respected, provided that we continue to concentrate european efforts on those who need them most. how can we pursue this cohesion policy? i would simply like to make two points: more efficiency, better management - i have spoken about this before the committee on regional affairs and transport and i will speak about this before the committee on budgetary control - but also a credible policy, with ambitions - i have just mentioned them - and with the means. we did not want to make any estimates or budget forecasts. it would be too soon and it would be putting the plow before the oxen. let us first talk about the objectives, the substance and the european priorities. then we must find the means to serve those priorities. the only thing i have noticed and want to say to your assembly is that in 1999 a number of things were decided in berlin. what has been decided is that in 2006, at the end of this programming period, when you add the resources allocated to the structural funds, the cohesion fund, the pre-accession appropriations - in particular ispa - and the appropriations set aside to deal with the first accessions before 2006, we end up with a total effort, when we add all that i have just said, equivalent to 0.45% of community gdp. that number is a reference point for me. this is an important figure in the financial discussions we will have together and with the member states when the time comes. ladies and gentlemen, whatever the level of expenditure in relation to the increase in disparities, i think i can say that cohesion policy will only be credible if it has the necessary resources. whatever the level of structural expenditure, it is also important that the content of the other community policies is also favourable to cohesion as recommended in the treaty. when analysing the contribution of other community policies, there is still progress to be made, particularly in finding better synergies. i am thinking of the common agricultural policy. ladies and gentlemen, thank you for your attention and before i answer your questions, i would like to say that this report does not contain firm and definitive guidelines. he doesn't propose decisions. he's opening the debate. it is a tool for information and evaluation, a picture of the union of 27 for the first time, and finally we ask questions and make some recommendations. we will be much more specific in the third report in 2004, which will be very shortly before the time of decisions on the new agenda. i simply think that this debate on cohesion policy and this report prove that cohesion policy has been effective and useful, in accordance with the objectives of the treaty. more european unity, greater solidarity between the peoples of europe, respect for the diversity of european territories. that is still our objective. this debate on cohesion is part of the larger debate on the future of the european union as it was opened in nice, and as we will be participating in it. we are not going to be able to do that in the next three years, before the new intergovernmental conference in 2004, when it comes to this great debate and when it comes to the particular debate on the idea we have of the european union, the idea of the level of solidarity between peoples and territories. i thank you and assure you of the european commission's commitment to the part your assembly will play in these debates. in writing. - (el) in the motion for a resolution with rapporteur mr e. ferri, the real need to combat organised crime provides a pretext for extending and activating state and interstate mechanisms of repression and for abolishing all forms of national sovereignty. the proposal calls for measures to be taken to eliminate the dysfunctions resulting from the application of the mutual legal assistance system, to strengthen the role of the court of justice of the european communities and to extend its powers, to set up the european public prosecutor's office, to speed up the procedures for the harmonisation of criminal law, etc., etc. these are institutions which flagrantly violate the national sovereignty of the member states and the fundamental principles of the conduct of the procedure, which date back to the french revolution. all this is being done in the name of the establishment of the so-called 'area of freedom, security and justice' as envisaged in the treaty of amsterdam and at the tampere summit. it should be added, however, that it concerns 'the new world order in the post-emu era'. in reality, for the peoples of europe, a space of 'lack of freedom, insecurity and injustice' is being gradually established. this is also clear from the fact that 'terrorism' (as understood, of course, by the drafters of the texts in question, who, for obvious reasons, never wanted to specify a vague legal concept before including it in the list of repressible crimes) is equated with organised crime. under the pretext of terrorism, they seek, on the one hand, to suppress all forms of political opposition, mass popular struggle and insubordination to their options, and, on the other hand, to make the reactionary measures they adopt more acceptable to the conscience of the peoples. but who says anything about organized crime and terrorism ? the massacre of the yugoslav people and the poisoning by uranium bombs of a defenseless population, but also of soldiers from eu countries, was this not an organized crime, which, moreover, has been recorded in time? indeed, could the repression of the protests in prague, nice and, more recently, davos and zurich be called anything but terrorist? how can we characterize the barbaric attacks on farmers, pensioners and the popular movement in general in greece and the other eu countries? we agree that organised crime must be tackled. but this mission cannot be carried out by the forces that use the mechanisms of state repression against the struggle of the popular movement, because these forces are led by imperialist organizations such as the eu and are responsible for the surge in crime, as well as the entanglements between organized crime and certain political formations and personalities (for example, the kla). we believe that the proposal is a new contribution to the establishment of the mechanisms, necessary for capital, for the repression of the rights and freedoms of peoples, that it tramples underfoot any notion of national sovereignty, that it contributes to the completion of the 'law of the new world order' and that it is dangerous for the peoples of europe. the kke meps will therefore reject it.

the commission is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet adopted a proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment. a directive which, as the rapporteur has pointed out, aims to incorporate into community law the so-called 'blue code' agreement, i.e. the code of practice for the safe loading and unloading of bulk carriers, which is already applied in the international maritime organisation, but which lacks the binding force that would result from its incorporation into community law. i would like to point out that we can accept most of the amendments proposed by the rapporteur and the various groups in general, and we can accept amendments nos 2, 5, 8, 10 and 15 in their entirety. and that we accept amendments nos 1, 3, 6, 7, 9, 11 and 12 in principle, but that the terms in which they are incorporated will have to be reviewed. however, we cannot accept amendments nos 4, 13, 14 and 16 for the reasons i shall explain. firstly, because, according to amendment no 4, the competent authority must have experience of loading and unloading bulk carriers. we cannot accept it, if we take account of the principle of subsidiarity. it is not appropriate to interfere with the responsibilities of the member states, which must have sufficient leeway to designate the competent authority which they consider best placed to ensure the proper application of the directive and to act as an arbiter between the master of the vessel and the terminal representative in the event of disagreement. as for amendment no 13, we reject it because it proposes to give the terminal representative the task of checking the conformity of the installation of the black box or the journey data recorder with the rules. ladies and gentlemen, the inclusion of this requirement in the directive is redundant, as the same provision is part of two other proposals for legislation on maritime safety, of a generic nature, namely the one on port state control in the erika i package and the proposal for a directive on a community system for monitoring, control and information on maritime traffic in the erika ii package. i would like to say that, above all, through this last proposal, we are going to ensure that black boxes are fitted on all community ships. at different stages, but all of them. in particular, to be clear, the intention is that black boxes on new cargo ships should be introduced from 1 july 2002 and on existing cargo ships, for those of 20 000 gross tonnage or more, from 1 january 2007 and for those of between 3 000 and 20 000 tonnes, from 1 january 2008. amendment no 14, which concerns an element of one of the technical annexes, is contrary to the code blue, the code of practice for the safe loading and unloading of bulk carriers, and the principles of the directive as regards the shared responsibility of the master of the vessel and the terminal representative for certain aspects of safety in the ship-to-shore relationship. finally, we cannot accept amendment no 16, which seeks to impose qualifications for crew which are not included in the stcw convention (convention on standards of training, certification and watchkeeping for seafarers), because it would discriminate against community crews. ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr van dam, and the whole committee for their work. this is one of the initiatives presented by the commission over the past year to increase the requirements, the requirements in maritime safety, which will allow us, although we cannot avoid all maritime disasters, because that is difficult, since there are always uncontrollable causes, to limit the risks enormously. as has been pointed out, over the last ten years more than 780 people have disappeared in accidents involving bulk carriers, precisely at community level. we understand that this is something we cannot simply accept when measures can be taken to ensure the safety of the crew's lives. we are not just talking about the problem of pollution of the seas, which we mentioned, for example, in the question of double-hulled oil tankers; we are talking about something that goes beyond that, which is the safety of crews on community ships and on ships that arrive on our shores. in addition, ladies and gentlemen, we are once again fighting unfair competition which sometimes arises with ships which are under regulations or with little care in checking and controlling everything that concerns the safety of the ship; we also have to take account of the way in which bulk carriers are loaded or unloaded. i think that with all these measures the safety package is properly focused on the community level, and we can say that the year 2000 was, in a way, the year of maritime safety from the point of view of the maritime sector. the other day we put forward in writing measures relating to documentation in the maritime cabotage sector; i hope that tomorrow the commission will give the green light to the port package; ladies and gentlemen, in this year 2001, we will continue to work in the maritime sector to give it all the impetus, all the dynamism and all the capacity that is needed, precisely so that it absorbs part of the demand for transport and is fully integrated into a more rational and more environmentally friendly intermodal transport.
the rapporteur. - mr president, the issues of safety in maritime transport and the protection of the marine environment from pollution caused by ships are of an unpleasant timing. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the european union currently has a large number of directives and regulations on these issues. at the same time, however, the european union must monitor and take a stand on legislation introduced by international organisations, and it must do so as soon as possible so that the member states do not show inconsistency with their international obligations. the european union must therefore rationally organise and concentrate its responsibilities in the field of maritime security policy. that is why the european commission is introducing this proposal, which contains two proposals: a proposal for a regulation and a proposal for a directive. the proposal for a regulation is designed to replace all the existing committees dealing with shipping safety and the prevention of pollution from ships by a single committee, the maritime safety committee. all other existing committees with a relevant content shall be abolished. the aim of this committee is to facilitate the implementation by the member states of amendments to the relevant international conventions. it is also important to stress that the maritime safety committee set up will operate in accordance with the regulatory procedure laid down in council decision 1999/468. as is well known, this procedure provides for and safeguards the powers of the european parliament and the member states. at the same time, however, the european commission has chosen a procedure which ensures that it has the right to judge for itself when an international amendment of regulations improves or deteriorates the level of safety in shipping. the procedure chosen to ensure this basic objective is summarised as follows: member states shall apply all the most recent decisions of international conventions, except those expressly referred to and contained in a community act. in the latter case, the european commission is immediately seized and either refers them to the maritime safety committee for blocking or does not refer them, in which case they will apply automatically. the committee on safety at sea is only referred to if it is considered that incorporating an amendment to an international convention into a community act would make the safety situation in the european union more unfavourable. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. finally, the proposal for a directive aims to include the committee on safety at sea in the directive, which is obviously necessary. the european commission's proposal is correct and is an attempt to rationalise and to better monitor community and international law on safety at sea and pollution prevention. the small number of amendments is intended to make the text clearer and thus to safeguard the rights of the european parliament in the regulatory procedure. finally, i would like to thank the members of the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism for their very constructive suggestions and for their unanimous support for my draft report.
- mr president, the setting up of the maritime safety committee under the pretext of seeking to improve the application of legislation governing the safety of ships, as well as the protection of the environment and the living and working conditions on board ships, reinforces the reactionary nature of the european union's policy in the maritime sector. the commission's proposals include reactionary arrangements, which mimic the role of international organisations such as the international maritime organisation and the world labour organisation. it is essentially a refusal on the part of the member states of the european union to take part in initiatives to accept or implement amendments to international conventions or resolutions, whereas they have every obligation as parties to international law. the european union, acting as a sentry for the interests of big european capital, is intervening in the change of international law that has been shaped since the war and, by becoming a global sentry, is sacrificing security, human life itself and the protection of the environment, on the altar of the increase in the profits of the monopolies. we are radically opposed not only to the basic direction of these choices but also to the piecemeal adoption of measures which are not effective but are intended to assuage the intense concerns and reactions of public opinion, the trade union movement and environmental organizations following the spate of deadly and highly damaging marine accidents. the european shipping centre is also trying to create its own mechanism for controlling maritime transport and imposing its own choices in the context of the trade war with the other shipping centres, the usa, japan, australia and others, which is tempting the international maritime organisation. it leads to discrimination outside generally accepted principles, which poses very serious risks to the real and effective safety of navigation. the main issue for the safety of ships, and in particular tankers, is the safety of shipping capital and the policy supporting it at national, regional and international level. this extreme lack of water not only persists but also expands. the situation of flagged vessels is a typical example. these vessels are directly linked to the deterioration of safety in shipping and are the main cause of most and most serious accidents. we believe that a policy aimed at protecting the safety of ships, passengers, crew and the environment must first of all effectively combat shipboard pollution, implement strict measures against the violation of legislation which is grossly violated in a cumulative manner and, above all, with impunity within the imo and international conventions. this policy must adapt the composition of the organism to the increased needs of ships, contribute to improving the living and working conditions of crews, safeguard the rights of the crew and the trade union movement and their involvement in safety matters.

commission. – the commission refers the honourable member to the statement given by commissioner wallström to the plenary session of this house on 17 january. since that date the commission has convened a meeting of the group of experts established under article 31 of the euratom treaty to give an opinion on the possible health consequences of the use of depleted uranium. the report of that group will be ready in a few weeks' time. the world health organisation will also issue a report on the subject in the near future. the commission will then consider appropriate action to be taken in the light of the scientific evidence produced by these activities. in response to the particular questions of the honourable member, the member states of the union have not informed the commission of their actions taken following receipt of the regional environmental centre report. the commission has no plans to initiate medical examination programmes. as the honourable member will know, it is a regrettable fact that the people of the region face a variety of threats to health from several sources and some of those threats are obviously environmental in origin. within the means at its disposal the commission is working with the united nations mission in kosovo – unmik – through the european agency for reconstruction, to eliminate these threats according to priorities for intervention.

commission. – mr president, the commission's attention was given to the use of depleted uranium in armour-piercing shells as long ago as 1993. at that time, the commission's reply to parliament related to the specific issue of the test-firing of shells in coastal waters, and it offered the view that such action would not have a significant impact on the environment. the commission continues to hold that view on the basis of the evidence that, in the marine environment, the only exposure pathway is the slow dissolution of uranium. it is important to note that the amount of uranium that could be dissolved is very small compared to the natural abundance of uranium in all sea-water. the movement of uranium away from the test-firing location by tides and currents could not, therefore, be distinguished from natural background concentrations. the commission understands public concern about the use of depleted uranium materials for military purposes. however, the commission draws attention to the fact that the firing of uranium penetrators at land-based targets has implications which are different from those of firing ammunition in the marine environment, because use on land can result in the dispersion of uranium oxides which may subsequently be inhaled, whilst that is clearly not the case in the marine environment.
- commissioner, from what i understand from your reply, the commission believes that there is no cause for concern and is not considering this case. am i getting this right ? is that what you mean ? that it is a matter of no concern to the public and that there are no dangerous levels of uranium in these seas?
commission. – mr president, it is not a question of the commission in any sense being complacent, either politically – because it is responsible to the public – or indeed scientifically. the points that i made in my earlier response related to the natural concentrations of uranium in sea-water of all descriptions and in all locations. on the basis of that scientific reality the feeling of the commission and all other scientifically advised persons and bodies is that the evidence of uranium deposit in sea-water is such that the firing of shells for target purposes in the marine environment does not make any significant addition to uranium concentrations. it is only on the basis of that widely accepted and well-documented scientific evidence that the commission takes its view. in addition, i did draw attention in my first answer to the strong distinction that has to be made between possible consequences of the use of depleted uranium material and munitions in a land environment where the oxides can be inhaled, and in a maritime environment where the consequences of the use of depleted uranium do not have the same consequences, since obviously no inhalation can take place.

in writing. - (el) the final wording of the new directive on genetically modified organisms (gmos) certainly has some positive elements, such as improved controls, public registers, the phasing out of antibiotic resistance markers, etc. however, it does provide for the possibility of issuing authorisations for large-scale cultivation and, in our view, does not fully apply the precautionary principle. during the debate, the major risks of gmos were highlighted without any definitive or convincing arguments being put forward against them. we are talking about the risks to public health (allergies, increased resistance to antibiotics, possible toxicity) but also, more generally, the risks to the ecosystems where gmos are disseminated (threats to biodiversity). in addition, the current knowledge of gmos is still generally insufficient, since there is no confirmed methodological understanding of their mode of operation and the evolution of all the genetic material that is introduced into the gene. there have been "accidents" or "surprises" in specific cases of such genetic interventions in the united states, australia and elsewhere. the council's common position was adopted despite the fact that member states such as france, italy and denmark considered it necessary to extend the moratorium for five years. all this reinforces our reservations and our conviction that, apart from the strengthening of controls and surveillance, the time is not yet right to legalise the voluntary release of gmos into the environment. moreover, we are not convinced that gmos contribute to agricultural productivity and development, or even that they contribute to any reduction in the use of certain plant protection products, if we refer to what is already being indicated by us research in a large number of regions where gm crops are grown. it is clear that a fundamental reason for the dissemination of gmos is the possibility for certain companies to be granted a patent and an exclusivity for the exploitation of gm varieties, as the italian minister for agricultural policy recently pointed out to the press in his country. for all these reasons, we cannot vote in favour of the common position on the new gmo directive.

the rapporteur. - mr president, the cotton sector is of great socioeconomic importance, as is well known, for spain and especially for greece. in greece, cotton is a national product, with over 300 000 families producing some 1 350 000 tonnes of cotton in objective 1 regions with an unemployment rate much higher than the community average and with an area per farm much smaller than the community average. the european union needs 4.5 million tonnes of cotton seed. this is a product in which the european union is particularly short of resources, since community production covers only 35% of its needs. the national guaranteed quantities are always provokingly lower than actual production, with the result that producers' incomes are wiped out by high co-responsibility fines, while paradoxically the community budget's expenditure is smaller the greater the excess of quotas. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not affected by the crisis, and that the community's agricultural policy is not affected by the crisis. however, instead of improving the existing regulation, as our cotton producers rightly demand, by adjusting the quotas to current production levels and adjusting, even partially, the institutional prices which have remained frozen since 1992, the european commission is not only proposing to maintain the current low aid system at the same level but is also raising the co-responsibility fine by 20%. the commission's objective of drastically reducing cotton production is unjustified because it refers to a product where the european union's self-sufficiency is only 30-35%. it is unfair because it is punishing the cotton producers for the reductions in international cotton prices which have been observed over the last three years, without them having any responsibility, while it has done nothing to reverse this situation. it should be noted here that the european commission, in order to support its unacceptable proposal, put it forward at the time when international prices were at their lowest in the last decade, in december 1999. today international prices have doubled. the european union also proposes to limit aid to cotton where it is most needed, i.e. when the united states of america is continually giving increasing amounts of aid to its cotton producers, thus allowing them to increase their production and reduce international prices. the environmental alibi is not justified, since the cultivation of cotton, as scientifically proven, is significantly less harmful to the environment than other similar subsidised crops that producers abandoned to cultivate cotton. the commission has also failed to take account of protocol 4 to the act of accession of greece, which establishes the cotton scheme to promote cotton production in the regions of the community where it is important for the agricultural economy. the cotton growers are asking for community preference to be extended to cotton, since the community is short of this product. cotton is the main plant-based textile fibre competing with synthetic fibres as a natural and healthier product, providing employment for hundreds of thousands of farmers and attracting young farmers to remain in the declining countryside. what do we suggest? we propose that all restrictions limits have to be set, these limits should cover the actual production of our producers at the present time; maintain the aid scheme; the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the cotton we are discussing today is actually produced in two countries of the european union, greece and spain, but it concerns the whole union because in all the countries of the union there may be industries processing cotton and producing yarn and cotton fabrics. so when we look at cotton production we have to keep both of these parameters in mind. another point worth noting in the case under consideration is the fact that community cotton production is extremely below its actual needs, barely covering about 35% of the needs. moreover, european cotton producers are small and mediumsized farmers with an average of 4.2 hectares per farmer, at least as far as greece is concerned. the production is therefore decisively determined by the small size of the holdings and by the soil conditions which correspond to the specifications of this crop. these objective conditions have led to a situation which, at the level of production, has been consolidated over the years in the following way: average annual cotton production in greece is 1,200,000 tonnes and in spain 350,000 tonnes. the report explains this in detail and its compromise proposal, in a prudent, measured and highly realistic way, asks the commission to recognise this reality by establishing a national guaranteed quantity of unginned cotton equal to 1,200,000 tonnes for greece, 350,000 tonnes for spain, 1,500 tonnes for each of the other member states. i think, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, that this responsible, realistic approach to the report must be accepted by the commission as it stands and must be enshrined in the forthcoming negotiations at the world trade organisation.
in writing. - (el) the commission's proposals on cotton are unacceptable for the following reasons: - the european union is running a deficit in cotton production; - the principle of community preference is being brutally violated; - the activity of hundreds of thousands of farm families is concentrated around cotton production; - cotton cultivation, despite the problems, may be less polluting than other industrial crops; - there is a gap between the guaranteed minimum quantities and actual production. it is therefore necessary to increase the guaranteed quantity so that it corresponds to actual production; the proposals of the european parliament's committee on agriculture are moving in the right direction, even if they are hesitant; - to take account of the inflation of recent years in the calculation of cotton prices; - to exempt small producers from co-responsibility levies; - to give trade organisations a key role in the quality assessment of the product; - to take special measures to support small producers.

mr president, the report by mr daul on the sugar regime is extremely careful and meets the requirements of all the parties involved, always within the limits and balances of the common agricultural policy. the proposals in the report serve the interests of community beet and cane producers in terms of employment and living standards to a fairly satisfactory degree. in addition, they meet the needs of production planning, since beet cultivation is known to be a crop which is part of a four-year cross-sowing with other large-scale crops. the interests of consumers are also protected in both price and quality standards. the expiry dates of the current arrangements are aligned with the berlin agreement. this adjustment is particularly important not only because all those concerned know the specific date of the regime change and will comply with it, but also because in view of the negotiations at the world trade organisation we have a specific offer to discuss. the sugar production from sugar beet scheme is a self-financing scheme. the main part of the expenditure of this scheme is in the form of export aid. the maximum level of export aid allowed by the wto is the level of 2000-2001. it would be a tragic mistake for the european union to change this regime before the time when it will be forced to change it, but demanding trade-offs. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the sugar sector is not left to itself to develop. and there is no possibility of making such investments if the investment environment does not have at least a medium-term horizon. we are therefore happy to support mr daul's report.
- mr president, the proposed sugar regulation is part of the suffocating budgetary framework agreed in berlin and aims to drastically reduce overall agricultural expenditure. in order to justify these financial cuts, an attempt is being made to paint the present situation in black in order to prepare the climate for drastic restrictions on quotas, prices and farmers' incomes, with the ultimate aim of drastically reducing community production. to avoid these social tensions which would be caused by the immediate application of such radical measures, the commission is working towards their gradual implementation, so that they are applied rigorously and comprehensively, with a new regulation to be adopted and to apply from the period 2003-04. in the context of this methodology, it is proposed to freeze nominal prices for another two years at the 1983-84 level, which means a reduction in real prices and incomes, whereas the prolonged freeze in sugar beet prices has reduced real prices by 33% in the period 1982-1999. the commission is also proposing to reduce quotas by 115,000 tonnes and to abolish stocks, in order to save the community budget eur 300 million a year. these proposals will have the dramatic effect of reducing real beet prices and producers' incomes. the further reduction of quotas, combined with corresponding reductions in all agricultural products, will increase the deadlock for farmers who, moreover, are not being given any suggestions as to which crops to grow. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full.

- mr president, commissioner, information is a basic principle of democracy. free access, transparency and dissemination of information on the environment are fundamental rights of every citizen concerned about the quality of the environment in which he lives and works. access to data held by public authorities and related to the environment is essential not only to improve public awareness but also to ensure citizens' trust and democratic participation. in the course of its implementation, directive 90/313 revealed a number of shortcomings which required its revision. the signing of the aarhus convention and the developments in information technology have led the commission to propose a new directive which largely covers the above points. the amendments tabled, however, are intended to broaden the commission's proposal even further, and it is necessary to include bodies and individuals acting on behalf of the public authority in the concept of public authority. the directive refers to information relating to the environment and must include all available forms such as written, visual, audio and other forms. any person or organisation may request environmental information without the need to prove a legitimate interest. as regards the time limit within which the administration must reply to a request for environmental information, i believe that two weeks is sufficient time for those concerned to be able to use the information in good time. i think it is very important that there should be no obligation to provide information, nor any exemption, on particularly important issues, such as those relating to emissions, discharges and deposits or other releases into the environment. it is also mandatory, whenever public authorities refuse to provide information, whether based on the exceptions or not, to give precise reasons for this refusal. the directive provides that public authorities may charge a fee for the provision of information. i believe that this fee should be limited to the real cost of reproducing the information, so as to facilitate public access to the information as much as possible. in conclusion, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mrs korhola, on her substantial report, which i am sure will be a major impetus towards active citizen involvement in tackling environmental problems.

commission. - in the light of the previous debate, i would like to tell mrs ferrer and other members of the house the importance of catalan which i was able to see on friday when i was in barcelona. i come now, if you will, to the question put to me by mr papayannakis about the major projects carried out under the community support framework in greece, and in particular in athens. mr president, the term 'large projects' has a very specific meaning in the regulations on the structural funds, since it defines a project with a budget of more than eur 50 million. in greece, since there are quite a number of projects of this type, which are co-financed by the cohesion fund or the regional funds, mainly in the transport, environment and energy sectors, it is difficult for me to give a very precise and complete answer in a few minutes. i would remind the honourable member that the granting of community co-financing for a project does not alter the fact that its implementation is the responsibility of the national authorities. and, of course, the information available to the commission is first supplied by these same authorities. naturally, we are interested, you can be sure of that, in the progress of these projects and the evolution of their cost. we are constantly concerned - and this is a constant concern - that the financial resources allocated to greece by the community should contribute to the maximum extent possible to the progress and quality of life of the citizens of your country, and this concern is the same for all the other member states. as for the major projects in your country, they fall into three parts, or rather three categories: those already completed, i am thinking of the athens metro, the spata airport, which will be inaugurated next may. some of these projects are carried out under and on the basis of concession contracts. a second category - i could have mentioned other projects under the first category - includes projects which in writing. - (el) no one could deny the need to take measures to prevent urban crime, juvenile delinquency and drug-related crime. nor can anyone disagree about the causes that fuel such crime. all criminologists now agree that the causes are primarily social: extreme poverty, unemployment, social inequality, the promotion of intense competition and the pursuit of maximum profit, which the world ruling class strives to impose as the supreme ideal, are among the factors that fuel crime. therefore, as long as the socioeconomic system based on the exploitation of man by man exists, crime has a bright future. but one would expect that some measures - social and economic in particular - would be proposed to improve the situation or, at the very least, to curb the rise in crime by prevention. instead, france and sweden, through their initiatives on which the report is based, propose the creation of a network for the exchange of information between member states on the measures they take in this area. the report also makes a scientifically inadmissible comparison between petty crime and organised crime. the prevention of ordinary crime means, first of all, the taking of measures aimed at mitigating the social causes that give rise to it, or even measures that counteract the opportunities to commit it. but in the case of organised crime, the emphasis is on repression. this assimilation is not accidental. it is once again evident that the need to combat ordinary crime is used as a pretext to curtail popular rights and freedoms and to set up an even more authoritarian institutional framework. let us not forget that the recent resolutions of the european parliament on organised crime contain measures to severely restrict all liberal civil rights in criminal justice. it is precisely in this spirit that a draft law is being discussed in greece these days, whereby the government, under the pretext of "the fight against terrorism and crime", is strengthening the mechanisms for suppressing the popular movement. for the reasons stated above, we as members of the european parliament from the kke (communist party of greece) will vote against the report.

the council. the council wishes to draw sjöstedt's attention to the fact that the schengen provisions do not regulate the issue of passports and identity cards for eu citizens. under the nordic passport union, which some of us strongly support, citizens of the nordic countries have the right to travel within the territory of the nordic countries without having to show a passport. the agreements on the accession of denmark, finland and sweden to the convention implementing the schengen agreement and the agreements between the council of the european union and iceland and norway concerning the association of those states with the implementation, application and development of the schengen acquis contain a provision stating that those agreements shall not 'constitute an obstacle to cooperation within the framework of the nordic passport union in so far as this does not run counter to or impede' the application of the agreements. in addition, the application of the schengen agreement is neither in contradiction with nor in the way of the nordic passport union. after the nordic countries have joined the practical schengen cooperation, citizens of the nordic countries will still be entitled to travel within the territory of the nordic countries and stay in hotels, campsites, etc. without having to present a passport. the application of the schengen acquis in the nordic countries does not therefore imply any obligation to present a passport. consequently, swedish citizens will be able to travel within the nordic countries without a passport after 25 march 2001.
the council. i believe that i have already answered the additional question put by mr sjöstedt. the somewhat complicated and lengthy answer i have given is based on a fairly thorough analysis of the situation. the simple conclusion is that no passport is required, so i can only repeat it. as we see it, it is therefore clear that the application of the schengen acquis in the nordic countries does not imply any obligation to present a passport.

the council. the member states have the competence to acquire and lose the nationality of a member state. although there are directives which contain certain rules on the content and validity of identity cards and passports, it is clear from the directives in question that it is the member states which issue identity cards and passports to their nationals in accordance with national law. it is for each member state to take the measures it considers appropriate to withdraw such identity cards and passports if it is subsequently established that the holder of the passport or identity card is not entitled to be treated as a national of that state. i can also say that the council has not asked for information on the naturalisation procedure in greece and that the council has no competence to take a position on any approximation of the acquisition of nationality laws of the member states. as explained just now, this matter falls within national competence.
