commission. – mr president, may i begin by saying that the commission welcomes this report on the operation of the european works councils directive. it will certainly form a very significant input into our review of the directive. in particular i should like to thank the rapporteur, mr menrad, for the effort he has put into this report over a long period. the report clearly reflects his profound knowledge of the subject. i am also conscious of the time that the committee on employment and social affairs has devoted to this issue and in particular the extremely useful public hearing which the committee organised earlier this year, giving all actors the opportunity to express their views. the report acknowledges the overall success in the operation of the directive, while identifying a number of weaknesses. the directive was the first piece of community legislation to provide for a specifically transnational aspect of employer/employee relations and in doing so it broke completely new ground. from that beginning we now have a situation where there are european works council agreements in 650 companies or groups in europe, covering about 60% of relevant employees. in each of those companies meetings are now taking place between management and employees from several member states. that would have been almost unthinkable some years ago and it is a considerable achievement. because of the inherent flexibility of the directive, this has been achieved notwithstanding the wide variation in employee participation and industrial relations systems in the member states. as the report acknowledges, the directive has undoubtedly contributed to the development of a european consciousness among employee representatives. in acknowledging the directive's success, i should also like to acknowledge, as the report does, the role played by the trade unions, in particular the trade unions at european union level. their input into and coordination of numerous negotiations has made a major contribution to the success of the directive. to acknowledge the overall success of the directive is not, of course, to say that there have not been problems with this application. the commission's report to parliament and to the council in april 2000 identified a number of legal and practical problems relating to the application of the directive. parliament's report, prepared in response to the commission's report, has elaborated on that analysis and identified a number of weaknesses in the operation of the directive and it proposes a series of changes to address those weaknesses. parliament may be assured that we shall examine all its proposals carefully. a key issue highlighted in the report, one which, as the report notes, has been the focus of much public interest of late, is the clarification of what we mean by information and consultation. we need to ensure that employees are informed and consulted at the right time, when they can still influence decisions. the report highlights some of the cases where, regrettably, employees have not been properly informed or consulted on major decisions affecting their livelihoods. since i share parliament's concerns on this issue and since i am determined to do all in my power to meet those concerns, i was extremely encouraged by the decision of the employment and social policy council on 11 june to adopt unanimously a political agreement on a common position on the draft directive establishing a common framework for informing and consulting employees. the common position was formally adopted by the council on 23 july. as parliament may be aware, the commission's proposal for this directive contained comprehensive provisions on information and consultation both as regards content and timing. i am pleased to see that parliament's report has references to the commission's proposal as a benchmark in this regard. i know that parliament is now actively working on its second reading of the draft directive to allow for its adoption as soon as possible. there will be issues to be resolved between parliament and council, most notably on the issue of sanctions, a subject which has been raised by a number of speakers this afternoon. this is an issue where the council's common position is furthest removed from the shared desire of commission and parliament to see effective sanctions: sanctions for failure to meet information and consultation obligations. notwithstanding this, however, i believe that the political will now exists throughout the institutions to reach final agreement on this text quickly, thus putting in place a comprehensive framework for information and consultation throughout the community. just as some of the issues raised in parliament's reports relate to the information and consultation draft directive, so others – such as the role of trade unions, the issue of training for employee representatives and the length of time allowed to negotiate agreements – are touched on in the context of the draft directive supplementing the statute for a european company with regard to the involvement of employees. i am happy that parliament has delivered its opinion on this text so promptly thus paving the way for its final adoption, i hope, at the october employment and social policy council. parliament's report acknowledges – and it will be clear from what i have just said – that the revision of the european works council directive is closely linked to these two legislative texts currently under consideration, that is to say firstly on information and consultation and secondly on employee involvement in the european company. i have always believed that, because of that linkage, we should focus on finalising those two texts before addressing the revision of the european works council directive. that remains the commission's strong view. but now, happily, we have the final adoption of those texts in sight and therefore it is my intention to be ready, as soon as the information and consultation directive is finalised, to address the revision of the european works council directive. the first stage of that process will, as indicated in the report, be a formal consultation of the social partners. before concluding, may i give some brief replies to two specific questions. firstly, a question by mr désir and mrs flautre on what the commission are doing to address the social aspects of merger control. in brief, the answer is that the commission has indicated its intention to examine the interaction between community competition law on mergers and the social consequences of such mergers. the second question was put to me by mr désir. he asked about the commission's plans as regards the timing of the revision of the european works council directive. i would reply that the commission plans to address the revision of the european works council directive as soon as the information and consultation directive is adopted. the first step would be consultation of the social partners which, if conciliation on the information and consultation text is completed by the end of this year, could be launched early next year. the commission has consistently taken the view that the timing of any revision of the european works council directive is inextricably linked to adoption of both the information and consultation directive and the final adoption of the european company statute proposal. in conclusion, may i once again thank parliament very much indeed for its in-depth and knowledgeable reports which will provide crucial input into, not only the commission's thinking, but also the thinking of the other actors in this important issue.
the rapporteur. the report on the application of community law must be regarded by all of us as one of the most important reports, because it is the one which reflects the real state of the european union, its real pulse, its connection with the daily life of the citizen. this report is the first to be drawn up for the new decade, which is also the beginning of the new century, and i think it could be the starting point for a more comprehensive approach to the problems connected with the functioning of the single market, the european union as a whole and the vision of integration. three new developments, namely the imminent unprecedented enlargement, the search for deeper and more political integration of the european union and the inevitable increase in legislative intervention, require a radical review of the question of monitoring the application of community law. the whole issue is inevitably linked to the revision of the treaties and must be included in the priorities of the next intergovernmental conference. the white paper identifies the problem and it remains to be seen how it will deal with it. without reducing the delays in transposing directives and without developing a uniform understanding of community law, there can be neither a single economic area nor the promotion of the concept of european citizenship. a national of one country who goes to work in thing is not that, but that there is no explanation in the commission's report as to why they were not implemented. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure are not straightforward, but there are setbacks and certainly not dramatic improvements to be reassured about. the general pathological picture remains the same. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and that the community's financial resources are used to the full. then, in the time that remains, i will try to come to some general conclusions. the best transmission rate is in denmark, with an average of 98%. as regards the infringement control procedures, it should be noted that the whole control system is based on four stages. the number of warning letters that are the first phase is significant. for the three countries with the highest number, france, italy and greece are respectively 236, 160, 154 and for the three with the lowest number, denmark, finland and sweden are respectively 40, 43 and 46. the number of reasoned opinions, which constitutes the second stage, is considerably smaller and shows that this procedure is effective for some states, and very effective for others. however, the relative reduction in the time taken to send letters of formal notice and reasoned opinions is a positive point in the effort to speed up the control procedure. as regards actions before the court of justice, a further significant decrease in cases of non-compliance is confirmed. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. firstly, we have a gradual reduction from phase to phase to 35%-40%. secondly, the order of the states in the scale of infringements is to be maintained at all stages. thirdly, in the third phase, the number of non-compliances for some countries is marginal, while persistence is observed from other countries; fourthly, the effectiveness of the control procedures must not be undermined by the fact that they are used by the member states as a means of delaying the time of transport, thus creating a de facto new transitional period. the fact that some countries with different systems consistently have the worst negative performance and some other countries the best positive performance means that breaches are fundamentally linked to a particular political will. the problem of the application of community law is therefore a political one, not a legal or technocratic one, as many believe. it conceals a conflict between national sovereignty and european power and reveals the degree of maturity of the european conscience. finally, the commission has not provided any analysis of the circumstances and reasons for the statistics. i do not want to take up too much of your time, but i would like to say a few words about the amendments. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. i do not understand mrs thors' amendment and i would ask for some clarification before it is accepted.

commission. – as a direct result of complaints from private citizens, the commission launched an investigation on dvd pricing and on the dvd regional coding system in june 2001. the citizens' complaints related to the fact that dvd prices were significantly higher in the community than in the united states and that, because of the dvd regional coding system under which the world is divided into six geographic regions, a dvd obtained from the us could not be played on a dvd player sold in the community. the position of the major film production companies is that the basic purpose of such a regional coding system is to allow them to protect their copyrighted intellectual property and the traditional pattern of releasing their films at different times in different parts of the world, as well as to protect against piracy. the commission shares the honourable member's concerns that the community's consumers may suffer as a result of the dvd regional coding system. it prevents them from benefiting from a broader choice of dvd titles and potentially cheaper prices. the commission is therefore investigating whether the dvd regional coding system is compatible with community competition rules. in this context all aspects relating to the establishment and the ongoing operation of the system are being examined. having sent requests for information to all companies concerned by the system, the commission is currently reviewing the first batch of material.
i would simply like to ask you, commissioner, whether the same phenomena are occurring in other products, that is, whether we have codings in other products, because the cultural industry sector is very sensitive, there is a lot of competition from america in which we are weaker, and we are all aware of the situation, but i know your sensitivity to competition issues and i believe that your research will produce results.
commission. – reliable figures on the impact of armed conflict on children are hard to come by. although data is available it is often context-specific, anecdotal or piecemeal. the same consolidated numbers – two million children killed in armed conflict, 300 000 children involved in armed fighting, etc, which were brought to the attention of the international community in the excellent report prepared by graça machel back in 1996 are repeatedly invoked without qualification, although they cannot continue to hold true. i first highlighted the serious lack of reliable data at my meeting with the special representative of the un secretary-general for children and armed conflict, mr otunnu, in december 2000. inspired by the discussion with him i took the opportunity of the seminar organised by the swedish presidency in norrköping in march this year to call for increased efforts to improve the knowledge about children in conflict. since that time echo has sought to build momentum for the initiation of a large-scale data collection project. the main purpose of the endeavour is to get an impression of the real scale of the problem because the lack of reliable data is both detrimental to our advocacy efforts and a major obstacle to improving our humanitarian response. but i do not want to initiate just another project resulting in a report which might never be used. numerous studies have already been conducted on children in conflict. but, as long as there are no common concepts and common definitions, a complex project seeking to establish hard facts has little chance of succeeding. therefore, rather than focusing on instant results, we are seeking to build broad international support for a comprehensive initiative on a data collection methodology which will enable comparisons across regions and over time. last month, echo participated in the seminar in florence – filling knowledge gaps: a research agenda on the impact of armed conflict on children – organised by the office of mr otunnu, unicef and others. the workshop succeeded in establishing a network of researchers from both the north and the south, who are ready to undertake research around four themes, one of which will be data on children affected by armed conflict. in parallel with this initiative, echo will continue the cooperation with unicef. as the un agency mandated with protecting and assisting children, unicef has the potential to play a central role in data collection. therefore the commission is encouraging unicef to develop a substantial international data collection project on children in conflict, using mr otunnu's research network as a sounding board. i am ready to make a political as well as a financial commitment to improving the availability of data on children affected by armed conflict as long as any new initiative includes general agreements on definitions, concepts and methodologies for the data collection. the goal is to create a system that enables people working in the field, from all different kinds of organisations, to feed information into a uniform system. this will also have a strong effect on the advocacy work in the countries in conflict because they will know that the numbers, the boys and girls we talk about, the real statistics that emerge from this operation, have been produced on their own home ground and are not something which may or may not be true found in a report.

in writing. - (el) the only positive element in the report of the temporary committee on the echelon interception system is that it admits the existence of such a system and the possible existence of other similar systems. but he tries to dispel the anxiety by underestimating his abilities and his wider impact. worst of all, the report seeks to legitimise the collection of personal and other data as a legitimate means, within the framework of the eu's foreign and defence policy, to serve the needs of the rapid intervention force, for the fight against 'terrorism', etc. it also considered that cooperation between the intelligence services of the eu member states was essential. in any event, it 'urges the member states to verify compliance with fundamental rights, as defined in the european convention on human rights and the case law of the european court of human rights, of their legislation on the activities of intelligence services and, if necessary, to make the necessary adaptations'. the report talks about 'democratic control and supervision' of surveillance activities and 'invites national intelligence surveillance bodies to attach great importance, in the exercise of their surveillance powers, to the protection of privacy, whether surveillance concerns national nationals, citizens of other eu member states or those of third countries'! it expresses some concern about industrial espionage, obviously to protect the superprofits of monopolies. in any case, the report recommends encryption of messages as the main protection measure. these positions are revolting to the extreme and are a provocation to the basic requirement of protection of privacy, trade union and political activity. they try to convince us that we have to live with surveillance, with electronic data entry under the schengen convention, under the supervision of europol, and they try to impose on each of us the obligation to protect. for all the reasons mentioned above, we as members of the european parliament from the communist party of greece voted against the report.
in writing. - (el) i voted against the motion for a resolution on the echelon system because, in my opinion, it underestimates the importance and role of a global interception system which is led by the united states and in which a community country, the united kingdom, participates. secondly, it contains findings which cannot be fully substantiated, such as that the system is not used for military purposes. thirdly, the way in which the resolution deals with the question of the legality of intelligence gathering leaves the door open to the persistence of a very dangerous indifference in this area. fourth, it legitimises the possibility for the united states to continue, subject to manifestly insufficient prior conditions, its espionage operations within the european union. fifthly, instead of calling for the approval of transparency and democratic control of the activities of the secret services, it flirts with the idea of establishing a "respectable" euro-scale.

the council. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, these questions are very close to the debate we had this afternoon on the g8 in genoa and everything surrounding the g8 meeting. i would remind the honourable members of the unanimous position of the council and the representatives of the governments of the member states on the question of the security of european council meetings and other events likely to have a comparable impact. the council recalled that one of the objectives of the european union is to maintain and develop the union as an area of freedom, security and justice. within such a space, citizens must enjoy the right to express their opinions freely and to assemble peacefully. however, these rights should be exercised under conditions which do not endanger the safety of all citizens and their property. in this connection, on 13 july the council and the representatives of the governments of the member states deplored the actions of those who infringe these democratic rights by inciting, organizing and participating in acts of violence at public demonstrations. in order to ensure the application of these principles, the council and the representatives recalled in the context of major meetings that not only must a dialogue be established with non-governmental organisations, the social partners and civil society, but that the possibilities offered by the legal instruments and bodies existing in the framework of the european union must be and will be used. i would like to recall, in particular, as commissioner vitorino did a little earlier, the provisions of the convention implementing the schengen agreement and the joint action of 26 may 1997 on cooperation in the field of public order and security. the idea is that the task force of police whatsoever to do with what the author of one question has called a joint police unit in the context of a crisis management action by the european union. on the occasion of two questions put by mr cooperation to ensure, on the one hand, the exercise of the right to freedom of expression and, on the other hand, to ensure that public demonstrations of this kind can and do take place in a peaceful manner. the fact remains that in any event the responsibility for maintaining public order in the territory of the member states of the union lies with the authorities of each of them. the council is counsel. - ladies and gentlemen, at ministerial level, the eurogroup is taking stock every month of the preparations leading to the changeover to the euro. at the meeting on 4 june, ministers, the president of the european central bank and the commissioner responsible examined the preparations for the introduction of euro banknotes and coins on 1 january. they welcomed the encouraging progress made in this respect, in particular the fact that consumers are becoming more aware. they reiterated their intention to intensify communication campaigns in the second half of the year to help ensure that the changeover proceeds smoothly. in this context, the ministers reiterated their concern to ensure that the conversion of all prices, tariffs and charges within the authority of their government would have no effect on prices or would have a minimum impact on consumers. ministers called on traders to follow up on the commitment to good practice they made in the joint declaration of the european consumer and trader representatives adopted on 2 april 2001 and to do everything in their power to ensure overall price stability when converting into euro and to clearly communicate this commitment to their customers. ministers also noted that several member states had stepped up price monitoring and that the competent bodies were vigilant in the face of price conversion practices. the ministers are of the opinion that the dual display throughout the dual circulation period is of crucial importance to enable consumers to check prices in both euro and their national currency. in this respect, they encouraged consumers to become more familiar with prices in euro and to continue to compare prices between competitors carefully, particularly towards the end of the year and during the dual circulation period.

counsel. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the treaty on european union states in its article 6, first paragraph, that the european union is founded on the principles of freedom and democracy, on respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms. article 3 (2) states that the union shall respect fundamental rights as guaranteed by the european convention for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms and as they result from the constitutional traditions common to the member states as general principles of community law. in this connection, article 3 of the european convention 'prohibits torture or inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment, and article 14 provides that the enjoyment of the rights and freedoms recognised in the convention shall be ensured without any distinction, in particular on grounds of sex. article 13 of the treaty establishing the european community states that, within the limits of its powers, the council, acting unanimously on a proposal from the commission and after consulting the european parliament, may take the necessary measures to combat discrimination based in particular on sex or sexual orientation.' in april this year, the european union adopted guidelines for its policy towards third countries on torture and other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment, thus confirming the central place occupied by this issue in its human rights policy. these guidelines provide the union with an instrument to intervene in cases of torture and to increase efforts to further strengthen compliance with international rules.

i need advice. - as stated earlier in response to the oral question h-0545/01 by the honourable member, regulation (ec) no 1049/2001 of the european parliament and of the council of 30 may 2001 regarding public access to european parliament, council and commission documents will apply from 3 december this year. until that date, requests from citizens for council documents will be dealt with as before on the basis of decision 93/731/ec on public access to council documents. each of the three institutions concerned is now taking measures to ensure that the new regulation is actually applied as from 3 december next. article 15 of the regulation requires them to set up an interinstitutional committee to examine best practice, to deal with any disputes and to discuss future developments in public access to documents. as for the council, the competent services are currently discussing the measures to be taken and the timetable for their introduction, in order to facilitate the exercise by citizens of the rights which are now more clearly conferred on them by the new regulation. the necessary measures will be taken to ensure that the date of 3 december is respected. as regards the various types of documents which may be consulted by the public, i can inform the honourable member that, under decision 93/731/ec of 20 december 1993 on public access to council documents, all council documents can now be consulted by the public. the decision whether or not to grant them to the applicant will depend on their content and, in particular, on the exceptional arrangements - that is, article 4 of the decision - and will be taken on a case-by-case basis after a thorough examination. however, article 1 (1) of decision 93/731/ec, as amended by council decision 2000/527/ec of 14 august last year, provided that where a request for access concerns a document classified as top secret, secret or confidential within the meaning of the decision of the secretary-general of the council of 27 july 2000, which in addition relates to security and defence matters of the union or of one or more of its member states or to military or non-military crisis management, the applicant is to be informed that the document in question does not fall within the scope of decision 93/731/ec. i would like to draw your attention to the fact that this provision has not been incorporated in the aforementioned regulation (ec) no 1049/2001, which will enter into force on 3 december. however, article 9 of the regulation provides that requests for access to sensitive documents as defined therein are to be treated separately. for example, such requests should be dealt with only by persons who have the right to inspect the documents themselves. regulation (ec) no 1049/2001 is based on the same general principle as the 1993 decision, namely that each requested document is indeed examined individually. access may be refused only in the light of the exceptional arrangements provided for in article 4 of the regulation. in its decision of 9 april on the making available to the public of certain categories of council documents, the council decided to make a whole series of documents, particularly those relating to legislative work, directly accessible to the public via the internet. the council thus anticipated the entry into force of the regulation as regards article 12 (3).

mr president, the proposal for a directive on the establishment of quality and safety standards for the collection, testing, processing, storage and distribution of human blood and blood components is extremely timely in view of recent developments in medicine and public health. the introduction of new rules is intended first of all to modernise the legislative framework to cover areas not covered by community legislation, by strengthening the requirements on the suitability of blood and plasma donors, blood testing and the establishment at community level of a quality system for blood donation centres. i would like to emphasize two points on which i have sought to improve the commission's text in order to improve safety for the donor and recipient of blood or blood components. i am talking about amendment no 33, which requires blood centres to have a medical degree as a minimum and preferably a specialist degree in haematology or a related medical specialty. the second important point is that today we are facing a growing blood shortage. in my country the demand has increased and about three out of ten patients are in need of blood and, despite the increase in the supply of blood from volunteers, the need for imports has increased. with amendment no 75 we are seeking to avoid making the ban on blood donation incentives absolute, because it is wrong, both medically and scientifically, to have dogmatism on such an important health issue. the desire to donate blood should not be expressed in an absolute way. member states and blood establishments should be allowed to cover the collection, testing, storage and distribution of rare blood groups and components, when and as needed, with incentives. i would like to congratulate mr nisticò on his really excellent work and ask him to vote in favour of amendment no 75, which, while respecting the principle of voluntary blood donation, leaves the member states and blood centres free to meet any needs that may arise and is therefore medically and scientifically perfectly correct.

the commission is aware of the difficulties facing the community in the field of the internal market. i will not fail to pass on to president prodi and my colleague christopher patten the issues that directly concern their responsibilities within the commission. for my part, i would just like to underline three points: firstly, i would say that i fully share the idea that the strategy on terrorism should not be a strategy divided among the pillars of the european union. there must be a global strategy which mobilises various instruments: conflict prevention policy, of course, the development of a political dialogue with the most problematic regions within the framework of the common foreign and security policy, of course, development aid policy, of course, but also, let us be clear, security policy and police and judicial cooperation policy. we cannot ignore the root causes of terrorism, but we cannot allow the slightest ambiguity to remain, and we therefore say that no cause justifies the use of terrorism and violence which takes innocent lives. and so the best way to combat terrorism in the name of democratic values is to use the weapons of the law, police cooperation and judicial cooperation. but it is obviously good to stress that there are some responsibilities which we ourselves must address: the question of terrorist financing, the need to apply strictly the united nations convention against the financing of terrorism by covert means, the need to adopt effective mechanisms against money laundering and the tax havens which financially support terrorist groups, the question of the united nations convention on the trafficking of arms, that is, the follow-up to the palermo convention on organised crime. they are multilateral ways of demonstrating, within the framework of the united nations, that the people who died on september 11 did not die in vain. that is, the states have learned their lesson and are prepared to give unequivocal evidence of strengthening their cooperation in the fight against terrorism. the second topic i would like to address is aviation safety: i am pleased to say that the council of transport ministers has decided that a working group will present on 15 october a first report, not only on the most immediate security measures being taken at airports and on aircraft, but also on the need to reach agreement, especially with our american partners, on the early definition of these measures to enhance air transport safety, and i would also like to say that these issues will already be put on the table by the representatives of the european union at the general assembly of the international civil aviation organisation which begins on 25 september. with regard to political decisions - for example, the two framework decisions which the commission has just adopted - it is quite clear that unanimity may seem a difficult obstacle to overcome in the council. the commission has already expressed its sympathy on several occasions for the idea that the decisionmaking methods in the third pillar should be streamlined and simplified. but more than unanimity, the commission's position on the fight against terrorism is a clear political position: no state should block, but no state should exclude itself from the fight against terrorism. the effectiveness of the draft framework decisions depends on their uniform application in all the member states of the union without exception, because we do not want the union to be a single haven for terrorist or criminal activities. and in this connection i hope that the appeals of this parliament and of public opinion will be sufficient to enable the necessary compromise formulas to be found for strengthening police and judicial cooperation, which are the legitimate formulas in democracy for fighting terrorism.
the council has been unable to reach agreement on the content of the proposal for a regulation on the common organization of the market in fishery products. these successes are not by chance. they are the result of a major political choice in favour of solidarity, a choice which has been reflected in the budgetary allocation of more than one third of community expenditure to economic and social cohesion. while the 2000-06 programming period is in progress, the commission adopted on 31 january the second report on economic and social cohesion in accordance with article 159 (2) of the treaty. in addition to a relevant inventory of studies and statistical information in the context of enlargement, the report contains conclusions and recommendations which invite discussion on the future of regional policy. economic and social cohesion is a reality that we must deal with now, because we must provide answers for the next programming period, that is, the one that will begin on 1 january 2007, when the enlargement to the new member states will have become a reality. in view of the statistical elements of the second cohesion report, one fact strikes the imagination. the challenge of enlargement is twice as great as the present one, since the figures show that regional disparities are set to double. in relative terms, the development and conversion delays will affect a greater number of regions and will be more intense when we are 27 member states. moreover, by a mere mechanical effect, a number of regions in the present european union will no longer be included in the list of regions in difficulty unless their real economic situation has actually improved.
commission. - mr president, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i am very pleased to take stock, as you requested, of the great debate we opened here on 31 january when i came to present the second report on economic and social cohesion which the commission had just approved. on this occasion, beyond this presentation, we have opened a debate which i said, and i want to repeat it to you, will be a sincere, objective debate, without any taboo questions, on one of the major community policies which is at the very heart of the image and idea we have of this european union, since it is the policy of solidarity. since then, the debate has been fuelled by new contributions. i am thinking in particular of the forum on cohesion on 21 and 22 may, organised here by the european parliament, in agreement with your president and with the help of my colleagues anna diamantopoulou and franz fischler. at the same time, negotiations on enlargement are progressing with all the applicant countries. as you know, enlargement is one of the main reasons for this debate. i would like to recall here, as a preamble, our concern to distinguish clearly between the accession negotiations, which are based on the application of the acquis communautaire to the new member states, and the reform of structural policies for the period after 2006. the debate to which i invite you is therefore about the period after 2006. there is of course no question of calling into question what the present member states receive until 2006 from the structural funds. your assembly, in asking the commission to speak, wanted to take stock of this debate. i would like to thank you for this opportunity and therefore briefly touch on a few points. first, the forum we opened on 21 and 22 may, which was a moment of real debate, preceding other moments. since 31 january, the presentation of the lessons of the cohesion report has been a priority for myself and for my colleagues in the regional policy directorate-general. you can be sure that i am taking part in this debate, week after week, on the ground. for example, last friday in helsinki, where i met the 20 presidents of the finnish regions, or tomorrow and the day after tomorrow in porto, where i will be attending a conference of the maritime peripheral regions, where we will be discussing this future cohesion policy. and so the debate must spread, it must be decentralised in such a way that a number of ideas, requests, evidence or guidelines are sent back to brussels, and first to the member states. this forum on 21 may was a very important and instructive moment for me. it brought together several ministers, several former and current prime ministers, with representatives of regions, cities, not only from the 15 member states but also from the candidate countries, and many very high level contributions. i would like to draw attention to three aspects of all these contributions and to the dialogue that minister neyts-uyttebroeck mentioned earlier, which i had in namur with the 15 ministers responsible for regional policy at the invitation of the belgian presidency. firstly, the interest aroused in all member states and candidate countries in the future of this policy after 2006 and the emphasis by many on the genuinely political dimension of european solidarity. it's not just about money. it is also the expression, minister neyts-uyttebroeck, which you have very well said on behalf of the presidency, perhaps the most concrete, precise and visible of the values on which the union is founded. the proof that this union is not only a large free trade area and a large market, but also a community of solidarity and that it must one day be a political power. secondly, i have not heard any negative reactions to the enlargement process. of course there is concern, but also a sense of the historic duty which falls to the union and the interest of both the new and the old member states in helping to ensure the sustainable and balanced development of the whole european continent. thirdly, i also felt that in order to make this debate a success, it was important not to forget the needs of the regions of the member states which are currently members of the union. and i want to reiterate my conviction here: just because we are going to bring the very poor eastern regions - estonia, slovenia, poland, bulgaria and others - into the union, that does not mean that the poor or the regions in difficulty in the north, the centre, the south, not to mention the outermost regions, will become rich as if by magic. there will be problems and needs in the present union after enlargement. this, ladies and gentlemen, is what ultimately provides a very strong incentive to maintain a cohesion policy adapted to the new economic challenges and the future geography of europe. my second point concerns the procedure for the future. as you know, the commission has undertaken, following the requests made by the council in june, to report regularly to the council on the progress of its work on the future cohesion policy. i am also obliged to inform the european parliament of this, as i am obliged to inform the council. i will propose to the commission that the first of these regular interim reports be adopted at the very beginning of next year, in january, as soon as we have new statistics which will clarify, update and clarify the information already in the cohesion report. i would like to point out that these new statistics, which are provided by eurostat, relate to gdp per capita in 1999, for example, or to unemployment statistics for the year 2000. so the more up-to-date the figures are, the more serious and objective the debate will be. this interim report for january will therefore contain an update of the figures and analyses in the first part of the report on cohesion devoted to the situation of the regions. it will also present the results of the various studies we have commissioned and the lessons learned from the major debates and seminars organised. i can give details of the additional studies ordered: we have ordered a study on the situation of the islands, as well as a study on the macroeconomic impact of the structural funds. in this macroeconomic study i shall also be very keen to provide you with information on the impact of these structural funds in terms of the direct effects of the commitment of these funds in the net contributor countries. i also intend to request a further study on the situation of mountain regions or regions with long-term natural handicaps. in order to feed this debate, seminars will be organised during the first half of 2002 on each of the ten community priorities identified in the cohesion report, with the participation of experts from the member states and regions whom we will invite according to the different themes. my third point, which is also about the future, concerns the substance of the debate. i would like to express or reiterate three convictions on the substance of the debate. my first conviction is that with the prospect of this enlargement of the union in sight, the member states and regions feel the need for more community cohesion policy rather than less cohesion. i do not believe that any form of deregulation or renationalisation of regional policy can be the way to meet this requirement, this need for cohesion in an enlarged union where there will be - and the figures speak for themselves - more disparities than there are today. but i am also firmly in favour of looking with you, as we reaffirm the need for cohesion and as we devise this new cohesion policy and this new regional policy, for ways of moving towards more decentralisation, simplification and less bureaucracy. as far as we can, i will be anxious to look for and propose all the ways of moving towards greater simplification and decentralization in the european procedures. my second conviction is financial. even if the financial debate itself is premature today, i am not the only one to consider, as i have already said here in my own capacity, that a financial effort that would represent 0.45% of the union's gdp is in my view a floor below which the credibility of future cohesion policy would be called into question. i think i can say again that, in my view, unless the credibility of this cohesion policy is called into question, it will not be possible to go below the threshold of 0.45%, which is a threshold generally accepted by the heads of state and government in berlin. my third conviction is that future cohesion policy must be fair and non-discriminatory. it must therefore address regions with very different structural difficulties and address issues of a heterogeneous nature, i.e. regions which are very lagging behind in terms of development and which are mostly in the candidate countries, the regions of today's fifteen which have not completed the process of real convergence and for which it will be necessary, in due course, to find a fair treatment, so that they are not penalized by a statistical or mechanical effect linked to the threshold or the new averages in the context of the enlarged union, and finally to address the difficulties of certain areas which have particular handicaps or certain social issues of concern such as equal opportunities or the situation of urban areas. in conclusion, mr president, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to remind you that the report we will be proposing at the beginning of next year will be based on the results of the major debates held in the second half of this year. this is just the first of the many dates i've asked you to go on. there will be more before i present the third cohesion report in 2004. it is in this spirit that i am fully available today and in the weeks ahead to listen to your suggestions, recommendations or criticisms.

disapproved of for his shortcomings, he feels an environment that embraces him, then all groups, and they are numerous, who are victims of harassment will prefer to enter the labour market and then the results of their offer will far outweigh any weaknesses. if we consider that, according to statistics, the number of people who make up such vulnerable groups exceeds 8%, we can easily see the political dimension. at the same time, the formation of corresponding groups of harassed victims has many origins, from women and people with disabilities to foreigners and people of other religions. this also shows us the qualitative dimension of the problem. based on this report, we should develop a plan to combat the full range of harassment, from simple irony, discrediting, intimidation and humiliation and undermining to more violent acts, a plan that will be applied regardless of whether it is downward harassment, from supervisor to subordinate, upward harassment or horizontal. the action plan should include, in addition to measures to prohibit harassment, an obligation on employers to impose a climate of non-harassment among colleagues, accompanied by measures to encourage such individuals at work. and since no one is bad, it must be accompanied by measures to remove suspicion and prejudice towards the individuals who make up these groups.
the rapporteur. - mr president, i understand that there is a statement from the commission on my report, which i would like to hear.
in writing. - (el) the famous economic and social cohesion is a deliberate attempt to divert the attention of the peoples of europe to a policy in the service of big capital. we are not alone in believing that under capitalism the inexorable law of asymmetrical development prevails: reality and the commission's own data in its reports confirm that we cannot speak of convergence but on the contrary of disparities between the member states of the community, between prosperous and less favoured regions, and also between regions of the same member state. a typical example is greece, where gdp in regions such as epirus, lesbos or the peloponnese has decreased instead of increasing. the per capita income in hamburg is 440 per cent higher than in epirus, the poorest region in the eu. in greece and portugal, 22% of the population live below the poverty line. eu decisions further exacerbate economic and social disparities by serving the choices of monopolies. the emu itself and the stability pact remove the power of the member states to exercise monetary policy, they impose budgetary discipline with cuts in social spending and public investment. privatizations and, more generally, structural reforms, which are fostered by market liberalization and reactionary decisions on the labour market, have particularly harmful effects on the incomes of the working classes and the country's economy. the predominance of atypical, temporary and precarious employment, parttime work and reforms of social security systems, combined with the concentration of capital, are aggravating an already intolerable situation. the structural measures financed by community funds are not aimed at genuine development of each region. they do not take account of the peculiarities of these countries, of their comparative advantages, but they are dictated by a large capital which demands increased profits and a more active contribution to the objectives of the stability pact. this policy is reflected in the allocation of large amounts of money to 'showcase' projects, the disappearance of traditional production sectors and the establishment of infrastructures that serve private capital, promote the liberalisation of markets and the full license of monopolies. workers are fighting against these choices and eu policies with their harmful consequences that the most disadvantaged layers, workers and pensioners, small and medium-sized farmers and small enterprises, mainly in the processing and trade sectors, are paying and will pay.
in writing. - (el) the asymmetrical capitalist development which characterises the eu economies is confirmed by developments in the last decade, during which the imbalance has increased between member states, between prosperous and less developed regions, and even within the same member state. instead of convergence and cohesion, we have a gap that keeps widening, creating a greater wealth-poverty polarization. the report itself acknowledges that the eu "continues to show profound regional disparities in economic and social development, and the regional disparities observed within some member states have even tended to widen". the dominant triangle (yorkshire, franche-comté, hamburg) concentrates half (47%) of the income, while it represents barely one-seventh of the area and one-third of the population of the eu, while 18% of the latter have an income below the poverty line. this is the result of the 'employment mobility' strategies which strengthen the concentration of population and wealth in the prosperous regions of the centre of the union, the flexible forms of employment, the unrestrained privatisations and the subordination of all policies, national and community, to the law of profit. instead of creating jobs, we have mass layoffs, with a sharp rise in unemployment. the report also notes that unemployment rates in the least favoured regions are as high as 23%. in the main recipients of structural funds - italy, spain and greece - a comparison of official unemployment rates in 1988 and 1998 shows a marked increase, mainly in greece, where they rose from 6.7% to 11.7% respectively, an increase of around 75%. these structural interventions were not aimed at the real development of each region, taking into account its specific features, but were subordinated to an increased profit for large capital. on the one hand, the resources allocated to structural assistance are insufficient and limited, and on the other hand, 30 to 40% of the overall funding allocated to the least-favoured member states goes to the most prosperous in the form of the purchase of know-how and capital goods. the cost of this financing has been the elimination of traditional production sectors, the change of crops, the destruction of agricultural products and the depopulation of regions. it is the less favoured layers of the population who pay the consequences, while the profit goes to the monopoly monsters and the various rodents around them, since the community funds are the "deniers" of eu membership, are in a way a certain form of compensation granted to big capital so that it largely lifts its protectionism within the framework of the national market.
in writing. - (el) the cruel reality of the enormous social problems affecting working men and women not only in practice, but also in the most abrupt way, denies all the proclamations about equality and the prosperity of citizens, it turns them into provocations and mockery. the first and main victims of the market and competitiveness policy are women. unemployment is hitting them hard. flexible jobs are freely offered to them, so that they are a cheap labour force, are paid less for equal work and face more problems of precarious employment and unequal access to the labour market; the report also highlights the importance of the role of women in the labour market and the need to ensure that they are not excluded from the labour market. social rights and benefits are seriously undermined and will be further undermined by the planned changes in the social security and pension systems, which will put even more pressure on women who have to perform several tasks at once, filling two or three roles. equal opportunities for women cannot be conceived in a society of exploitation, a society of unemployment, of underemployment, without a fair social perspective, without a welfare state. equal access opportunities for women must be ensured, legislation on the protection of occupations with an unhealthy environment, maternity protection must be strengthened, and measures must be adopted to ensure all forms of atypical work. only through their struggles will the workers be able to defeat the anti-social policies that are being imposed, and only then will they be able to improve their lot. only in this way will the place of women in society also be improved. and in those struggles, we stand with them.
in writing. - (el) the report provides a wealth of information on how the schengen convention has actually worked in the ten countries where it is fully applied and it substantially supports our views on its arbitrariness, undemocratic and centralising character. in concrete terms, he admits that it contains gaps and omissions in terms of transparency and democratic control, and even violations of the famous eu charter of fundamental rights, an opacity of the role of europol, the lack of information from the european parliament, etc. it is particularly revealing in the case of the schengen information system (i.e. the computerised register), where 89% of personal data relate to persons 'referred for non-admission'. documents that should have been destroyed are used to complete the police archives, data are kept "on persons whose identity is misused without informing the legal identity holder", while "there is an absence of appropriate criteria for data entry, especially in the case of persons reported for non-admission purposes". on the free movement of persons, the report criticises the uk and ireland for maintaining internal border controls and france for carrying out checks on people coming from the netherlands because of dutch drug legislation, but does not mention the checks and entry bans imposed in france and sweden on eu citizens who came to take part in the nice and gothenburg protests. but it is genoa that offers the most representative picture of what schengen is: on the one hand, restrictions on free movement with harassing police checks and entry bans, and on the other, a total or even abusive application of computer logging and police cooperation (european police). all this confirms that the schengen convention is the instrument that facilitates the work of the euro-repression, the fabrication and tracking of suspects, the intimidation of popular movements. the situation has become even more dangerous since the witch-hunt launched by the united states, with the help and tolerance of the eu, under the pretext of the tragic events of 11 september; in the name of fighting "terrorism", they seek to stifle any free voice that fights or even challenges their unpopular policies. the report makes a number of technocratic proposals (further information to the european parliament, adoption by the council of a binding legal framework on the protection of personal data, strengthening of the role of the joint supervisory authority). but with that, the report calls for the establishment of a community information system, in other words a single computer network for schengen, europol and customs data, with a view to even more efficient record keeping, and this in the name of freedom, security and justice! for the reasons i have just mentioned, we as members of the european parliament from the communist party of greece voted against the report.

commission. – mr president, in reply to the oral question by mr alavanos, the letter of formal notice sent to greece to which the honourable member refers, concerns a recently initiated infringement procedure regarding the failure of presidential decree no 165 of 23 june 2000 to comply with directive 89/48 in certain areas. on the other hand, the procedure referred to in question e-1336/01 earlier more specifically concerns problems surrounding the misapplication of directive 89/48 to the psychology profession. the commission considers that diplomas awarded in greece by another member state and the refranchising agreements do not relate to training provided from or by greece, but to training provided from or by the other member state, since the greek establishment in which the training in question has been provided is merely an intermediary without any autonomy. in other words, it is not the greek establishment that awards diplomas, but rather the university in the other member state, and consequently, if those diplomas relate to professional training within the meaning of directive 89/48, they are covered by that directive. the purpose of the letter of formal notice is to obtain the member state's observations on specific questions. greece has not yet replied to that letter of formal notice and, therefore, no firm grounds for infringement procedings have been established as yet. consequently, the commission is not able to reply to that part of the question from the honourable member. given the similarity of the subject matter, i shall now continue, if i may, by answering the question by mr hatzidakis. in the judgment to which the honourable member refers, the court of justice of the european communities condemned greece for its failure to communicate measures taken to implement directive 89/48 – the directive i referred to in my reply to the previous question. the commission thus initiated a new infringement procedure against greece with a request for the fixing of penalty payments. this second procedure was closed when greece complied. it complied with the court's judgment by forwarding presidential decree no 165 of 23 june 2000 to the commission. as i pointed out in my reply to the previous oral question put by mr alavanos, the commission considers that certain provisions of directive 89/48 have not been implemented correctly. the commission has also received complaints from individuals who, even since the implementation of the directive, have not seen their rights respected. the commission has, therefore, initiated a new infringement procedure, this time for incorrect implementation and application of the directive, and the letter of formal notice to which i referred just now has been sent to greece. we expect to receive the observations of the greek authorities very soon. a specific procedure for recognition of the psychology profession is currently being introduced, and that profession, together with a number of others, has been the subject of specific implementing legislation on problems concerning the application of directive 89/48 that have also been drawn to the commission's attention in that context. that is my reply to the question put by mr hatzidakis.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his detailed and technically sound reply. it remains to be seen what the government's response to the commission's move will be. as the commissioner knows that i have raised this issue many times, i just want to inform him that the public response of the education minister is that i am asking the wrong questions to the wrong commissioners and getting the wrong answers. i am therefore awaiting the greek government's reply. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european parliament is able to exercise its right of initiative in the matter of the internal market, and i would like to ask the commission to take this into account in its proposal for a directive on the internal market for electricity.
commission. – i can assure mr alavanos that the commission has looked at this whole issue in the broadest possible sense and has not confined itself to the matter of psychologists. the basic issue is the question of whether a degree awarded by another member state, following a course of studies which takes place physically in greece, is or is not assimilated to a similar degree issued in greece itself following studies at a greek university. so it is the equivalence between a purely greek degree and a degree awarded by a university of another member state, but where the study has been undertaken at a greek establishment. that assimilation, that equality is the purpose of directive 89/48, to which i have referred at various times this afternoon. the commission is taking that into consideration and has asked greece to explain why that directive has not been transposed and applied accordingly. the directive does not confine itself to psychologists and, to that extent, i can assure mr alavanos that his fears are unfounded.
the commissioner must know that the issue we are talking about is not just a matter of some citizens of greece, but of thousands of graduates of european universities who, because of the greek state, which has a crazy education system, in my view, are sent abroad to study and then not recognized for their diplomas. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and i am sure that the commission will take the necessary steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. i would like to say that i am glad that you have found that the court's decision has been poorly implemented and i would like to ask you, because the greek people are listening to us, what the legal consequences will be if the greek government persists in its view? what will the commission do next? just to make it clear to the citizens.

mr president, one of the aims of the directive is to improve access to public transport for people with reduced mobility. the european parliament and the council were sharply divided on this last point. the european parliament's insistence on drawing up provisions to facilitate access for people with reduced mobility, and in particular for wheelchair users, was justified, and so was the rapporteur, mr miller. the agreement reached by the conciliation committee, in line with the european parliament's position as always, proves once again that important issues such as quality of life can only be advanced by bold policies, unfettered by the conservative clutch of technocratic and economic approaches. the proposed directive broadens the definition of 'passenger with reduced mobility' to include all passengers who have particular difficulties in using public transport, in particular the elderly and people with disabilities. reduced mobility does not necessarily imply some form of medical problem. this is an important innovation which must undoubtedly be the starting point for other corresponding adjustments if quality of life policy is to have any substance. in particular, disability can take various forms, including not only physical, motor, but also intellectual and sensory disabilities. so, it includes people who are just walking, short, pregnant women, etc. it also states that the inclined parts of vehicles should have an anti-skid surface. it is also interesting to note that it is not limited to vehicle specifications, but also provides for the facilitation of access through the local planning of infrastructure in order to achieve the effect of safe boarding. this should guarantee safe level boarding for all persons with reduced mobility. i would like to believe that the spirit of the agreement reached will prevail in other similar efforts.

counsel. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i shall answer a number of questions very quickly, and unfortunately only briefly, because i cannot stay for the whole sitting. i should like to begin by giving a number of additional information on the convention. several members spoke on this subject. i think it is important to say that the principle of convening a convention to prepare the igc is, i think, taken for granted, since that is the question that was put to me. the participation of representatives of governments, the commission, national parliaments and the european parliament in the convention, i.e. a four-party composition, is also a given, of course. this participation could take the following forms: representatives of governments - 15 representatives were mentioned - , 16 members of the european parliament, 30 members of national parliaments and one representative of the commission. however, i shall not start a debate on the figures here. what is important to keep in mind is that the number of participants at this convention is to be relatively small. it must not be exponential: if we want to please, we can do so, but then we will end up with a convention that will not be operational. i think we should keep that aspect in mind. the laeken european council will probably appoint - and this is the direction we seem to be heading - a president, assisted by a bureau composed of at least one member from each of the components of the convention and assisted by a secretariat. the convention would not have the mandate to adopt a definitive text, but at this stage - i think it was mr barón crespo who asked the question - are these proposals, are these options? - at this stage, i say, we are in favour of options rather than proposals, except, of course, for the authorities to make a choice among the options, since options are always only variants of proposals. i am not advocating any particular view, but simply stating the facts. if it were up to me, we would go much further than proposals. it would be more radical. but i have to adjust to the presidential costume... i therefore report what i think you should report in order to act in a transparent manner. i can't do any better than that. the work of the convention should begin as soon as possible, under the spanish presidency, i.e. in the first half of 2002. i would also like to mention two points about this convention: it is important that you not only remember them, but also that you can discuss them and express your views on the subject. the association of the candidate countries is an important element. a fairly strong convergence emerged at genval on the need to associate the candidate countries with the convention. if there is indeed a tendency to give them a status, with a right of speech, it is obvious that the precise arrangements for their representation still need to be determined. the presidency proposes three possible formulas: either a representation modelled from the outset on that of the member states, namely one representative from each government and two members from each national parliament - one might imagine this; one representative per country for the duration of the convention; the third possible formula is a progressive representation, consisting of a single representative until the signature of the accession treaty and then a representation aligned with that of the member states after the signature. a representative of the economic and social committee and a representative of the committee of the regions could also be invited as observers. those are the ideas on the table. how do we involve civil society then? the idea of a civil forum was generally welcomed by the delegations. some questions were however raised, about the composition - the selection of those who would represent civil society - of this forum, about its organizational modalities, about the nature of its links with the convention - we are thinking for example of hearings, a whole series of formulas, of bridges of this type. the presidency is of the opinion that this forum can be conceived in two ways. either an enlarged assembly, in which case its exact composition, role and mode of operation would have to be defined, or a structured network, composed of organisations representing civil society, set up at european level. the organizations forming part of this network would receive official information on the work of the convention and their written contributions would form part of the official documentation sent to the members of the convention, they would be heard by the convention, according to modalities to be defined, and they could also, if necessary, be consulted by the convention on specific issues. if we were to go down this road, it would probably be appropriate to appoint a coordinator at political level, who could be chosen either by the laeken european council or by the convention itself. that is the supplementary information i wanted to give you on the convention, so that, of course, any ideas which might suggest or inspire you may reach us, since that is the purpose of this kind of exchange. so i am totally open to any comments and ideas, any suggestions on the subject. several of you have spoken about security in brussels. so i'm going to echo their concern. i think there are no difficulties in principle, except for one aspect, where we have some difficulties - i will try to analyse the matter and look at things more closely - one aspect, which is the problem of unconditional access to belgian territory: i am not convinced that this will not pose a number of problems. the rest of the matter, it seems, can certainly be settled fairly quickly. i promise to speak to the ministers of the interior and justice as soon as i return so that i can give you as soon as possible a concrete follow-up to the questions you have put to me. i want to highlight something that has come out of our discussions and that i am very much aware of myself. the commission's proposal is to be accompanied by a report on the implementation of the european union's policy on terrorism. mr barón crespo said so and mr lannoye drew our attention to it. i have already heard some people in this house say that they have not understood this very well: if i were to put mr berthu's programme into practice, it would obviously lead straight to a society that would kill freedom, which would have nothing to do with, excuse me for saying so, the quality of life, the democratic quality of our societies. that's the way it is, that's my opinion, and i share it. i think we have to pay attention to it, because it's really something important. we cannot, under the guise of fighting terrorism, revive old social illusions. i think that would be very dangerous, and i am not in favour of it. i think the difficulty we will always have is to constantly assess our room for manoeuvre. but please, let's not give in to security phobia, because we'll all fall victim to it sooner or later, and democracy will lose out. (applause) i would like to thank mr lannoye has stressed - and others have also stressed - something very important in terms of our foreign policy and i fully share his view in this respect: the enemies of our enemies are not necessarily our friends. i agree with you, of course. it is true that under the guise of this kind of consensual coalition, based on the immediate, necessarily, and, to a certain extent, it must also be said, on emotion, which is justified, one can, of course, when one engages in such a process, be tempted to conceal, not to see or to forget a number of elements. i believe that yesterday, at the foreign affairs committee, i was asked, for example, 'have you asked your interlocutors about state terrorism?' i think we must not lose sight of this aspect of our approach and our commitment to europe, of our values. it is true that we must also be attentive to this, in other words we must not forget that sometimes countries, realities and entities which are part of the coalition should also be held to some extent accountable for certain practices, behaviour and policies which they carry out. i therefore acknowledge that we must avoid any kind of angelicizing in this respect and not forget to continue our fight and our proselytizing for a democratic society wherever possible in the world. i therefore agree to increase the role of the international criminal court. as the days go by, we realize that it is absolutely essential to have this vital tool. i am asked whether i am in favour of the charter of fundamental rights being included in a constitution. everyone knows my position on this. unfortunately, i don't think all minds are mature enough yet. however, i think that everyone has their own arguments and that this debate must therefore continue. sooner or later, we'll get there. another important point: i was asked about the danger of liberticide. it is true that if a series of measures are taken - we have spoken of the freezing of assets and a number of other measures - it will be necessary to ensure that these measures, even if they are taken in an emergency, are nevertheless accompanied by a systematic evaluation, preferably shortly after their adoption, whatever their nature. it seems to me that it will naturally be necessary to review the measures taken in an emergency fairly quickly and fairly regularly. it will be necessary to avoid that, as is often the case in certain countries, such as belgium, whose practice i know, temporary measures are maintained after 50 years. i therefore support those who are asking for guarantees. if measures are taken in an emergency, there must be a mechanism for periodic reassessment of these measures. i would like to say that i agree with the majority of the ideas and concerns that have been expressed. mr dupuis spoke of the efficiency of the secret services. i have to tell you, i don't know much about the secret service, because they're secret. i suppose from that point of view they are at least effective. i simply call for more regular exchanges of information, more integration and better coordination. i think we can agree on that. mr dupuis, i also replied to you about the risk of encouraging state terrorism, or at any rate of concealing certain facts about certain countries. i also agree that we must not give up our action, our perseverance, our persuasion work to bring some countries to democracy. we must not ignore this fact or pretend that this problem no longer exists in a number of arab countries. but we must keep talking about it. i am personally convinced that political dialogue must be strengthened wherever possible, and political dialogue without taboos. it is, in my view, quite possible to impose it in our relations. this is a minimum requirement and, for example, during my visit to iran, we agreed to set up a group between the european union and iran which would put human rights issues exclusively on its agenda. this has been accepted. such initiatives should certainly be multiplied. ladies and gentlemen, mr president, i think i have been as complete as i can be. i may have
